are all distinct, i.e., for any two locations
l,l^{\prime}\in L
, it holds that
\Lambda_{l}\neq\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}
. We note that our analysis can be extended naturally to the setting when there are ties.

To prove this claim, let
\bm{\sigma}^{G}=(\sigma_{l}^{G})_{l\in L}
denote the allocations of the greedy algorithm and
\bm{\sigma}^{*}=(\sigma_{l}^{*})_{l\in L}
denote the revenue-maximizing allocation. Further, suppose for contradiction that the greedy algorithm is not optimal, i.e.,
\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{G}\Lambda_{l}<\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\Lambda_{l}
, where we have dropped the fine
k
from the objective of the linear Program (
10a
)-(
10c
). Note that this implies that there is some location
l
such that
\sigma_{l}^{G}\neq\sigma_{l}^{*}
, which consequently implies by the nature of the greedy algorithm that there is a location
\tilde{l}
such that
\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{G}>\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}
. However, by the feasibility constraint that
\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}\leq R
and the optimality of the revenue-maximizing solution it must hold that there is some location
l^{\prime}
such that
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{G}<\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}
.

To derive our desired contradiction, we now construct another feasible strategy with a strictly higher objective than the strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
. In particular, consider
\epsilon>0
as some small positive constant such that
\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon\leq\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{G}
and
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\geq\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{G}+\epsilon
. Then, define the strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{l}^{\prime})_{l\in L}
, where
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\sigma_{l}^{*}
for all
l\neq l^{\prime},\tilde{l}
,
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}=\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon
and
\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{\prime}=\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon
. Note that this new strategy is feasible. In particular, it is straightforward to check that
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}\in[0,\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}]
for all locations
l
and that the resource constraint is satisfied as:

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\sigma_{l}^{\prime}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\prime}+\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\sigma_{l}^{*}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon+\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon=\sum_{l}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq R,

where the final inequality follows by the feasibility of
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
.

Next, we show that the revenue of the new strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
is greater than that of
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
. To see this note that:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{\prime}\Lambda_{l},

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\sigma_{l}^{*}\Lambda_{l}+(\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon)\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}+(\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon)\Lambda_{\tilde{l}},

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\Lambda_{l}+\epsilon(\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}-\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle>\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\Lambda_{l}=Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}),

where the inequality follows as
\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}<\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}
due to the nature of the greedy algorithm which allocates resources to locations in the descending order of the values of
\Lambda_{l}
. The above relationship implies that the strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
achieves a greater revenue compared to
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
, a contradiction. Thus, it follows that Algorithm
1
cannot have a strictly lower objective than the optimal algorithm, implying its optimality, which establishes our claim.

B.2
Proof of Proposition
1

Given the best-response function of the users at a given location
l
in Equation (
5
), we prove Proposition
1
using two intermediate lemmas. Our first lemma establishes that, without loss of generality, it suffices to restrict our attention to administrator strategies where the total allocation of security personnel
\sigma_{l}
at any location
l
does not exceed
\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
, i.e., establishing the first point in the statement of Proposition
1
.

Lemma 3

(Upper Bound on Administrator’s Mixed Strategy Vector)
.

Suppose that users at each location best-respond by solving Problem (
1
). Then, there exists an administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
that satisfies
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\leq\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all locations
l
and is a solution to the Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
).

For a proof of Lemma
3
, see Appendix
B.2.1
. Lemma
3
establishes that allocating more than a certain threshold of resources, as specified by the fraction
\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
, at any location
l
will not improve the administrator’s payoff. Such a result holds as, by Equation (
5
), allocating
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
is enough to deter users from engaging in fraudulent behavior at a given location
l
; hence, allocating any more resources than this threshold at a given location will not result in a further improvement in the administrator’s payoff (see right of Figure
1
).

Our second intermediate lemma establishes the existence of an optimal administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
such that for at most one location
l^{\prime}

\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right)
, thereby establishing the second condition in Proposition
1
.

Lemma 4

(Structure of Optimal Mixed Strategy Solution)
.

Suppose that users at each location best-respond by solving Problem (
1
). Then, there exists an administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
that is a solution to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) such that for at most one location
l^{\prime}

\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right)
. For all other locations
l
, it holds that either
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=0
or
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
.

For a proof of Lemma
4
, see Appendix
B.2.2
. Using the structure of the optimal administrator strategy established in Lemmas
3
and
4
, it is straightforward to see that the optimal administrator payoff satisfies the relation in the statement of Proposition
1
. To see this, let
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
, and
l^{\prime}
be as defined in the statement of Proposition
1
. Then, we have that:

\displaystyle P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in L_{2}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l}+(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})y_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L_{2}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l}+(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})y_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L_{2}}p_{l}+(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})p_{l^{\prime}},

where (a) follows from the fact that
y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=0
for
l\in L_{1}
by Equation (
5
) as
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all
l\in L_{1}
and (b) follows as
y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=1
for
l\in L\backslash L_{1}
and the fact that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=0
for all
l\in L_{2}
and that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right)
.

Using the above inequality, we have the following relation for the payoff under the strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
:

\displaystyle P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}p_{l}-P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}),

(11)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}p_{l}-\left(\sum_{l\in L_{2}}p_{l}+(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})p_{l^{\prime}}\right),

(12)

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}},

(13)

which establishes the desired relation for the administrator’s payoff under the optimal strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
, thereby proving our claim.

B.2.1
Proof of Lemma
3

Suppose that
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
is a payoff maximizing strategy of the administrator and
\mathbf{y}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
is the corresponding best-response of users given by Equation (
5
), i.e.,
(\bm{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{y}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}))
is an optimal solution to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
). Then, we define a new feasible strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
that satisfies
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\leq\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all locations
l
and show that it achieves the same payoff as the strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
using users’ best response function given by Equation (
5
). In particular, define
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\min\{\sigma_{l}^{*},\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\}
for all locations
l
.

It is straightforward to check that
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
is a feasible strategy, i.e.,
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}\in\Omega_{R}
. To see this, note that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\min\{\sigma_{l}^{*},\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\}\geq 0
for all locations
l
as
\sigma_{l}^{*}\geq 0
by its feasibility to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
). Furthermore, we have that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\min\{\sigma_{l}^{*},\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\}\leq 1
as
\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\leq 1
for all locations
l
. Finally, since it holds that
\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq R
by the feasibility of
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
), it follows that

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\sum_{l\in L}\min\left\{\sigma_{l}^{*},\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\right\}\leq\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq R,

which establishes the feasibility of strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
for Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
).

Next, to see that
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
achieves the same payoff for the administrator as
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
, we first note that the best-response strategy of users at each location is unchanged under
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
and
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{y}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=\mathbf{y}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
. To see this, we show that
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
for all locations
l
by considering two cases: (i)
\sigma_{l}^{*}<\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
and (ii)
\sigma_{l}^{*}\geq\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
. Note that in the first case,
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\sigma_{l}^{*}
and thus it must be that
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=1
. On the other hand, in the second case, note that
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=0
and that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
, from which it follows by Equation (
5
) that
y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=0
as well. Having shown that
\mathbf{y}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=\mathbf{y}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
, we now obtain that:

\displaystyle P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}^{*}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L:y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=0}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}^{*}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in L:y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=1}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}^{*}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L:y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=1}(1-\tilde{\sigma_{l}}^{*})y_{l}^{*}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})

where (a) follows by splitting the sum, (b) follows from the fact that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\sigma_{l}^{*}
for all
l
where
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=1
, as in this regime
\sigma_{l}^{*}<\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
by Equation (
5
), and (c) follows from the fact that
\mathbf{y}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=\mathbf{y}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
.

Finally, by the optimality of
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
and from the above obtained relations for the new administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
, it holds
P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma})
, for all feasible
\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}
. Thus, we have established our claim that there exists an administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
that satisfies
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\leq\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all locations
l
and is a solution to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
).

B.2.2
Proof of Lemma
4

To prove this claim, we show that any administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
can be transformed into another strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
satisfying the condition in the statement of the lemma with at most the same payoff as that corresponding to
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
.

To see this, consider an optimal administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
such that the strategies
(\bm{\sigma}^{*},\mathbf{y}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}))
are an optimal solution to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
). Further, suppose that there are at least two locations
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
such that
\sigma_{l_{1}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l_{1}}}{d_{l_{1}}+k}\right)
and
\sigma_{l_{2}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l_{2}}}{d_{l_{2}}+k}\right)
. In particular, let
L^{\prime}\subseteq L
be the set of locations such that for any
l^{\prime}\in L^{\prime}
it holds that
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right)
. Then, we order the locations in the set
L^{\prime}
in descending order of the payoffs
p_{l}
and without loss of generality number these locations
1,\ldots,|L^{\prime}|
. Then, we construct
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
through a water-filling approach. To elucidate this approach, we begin by transferring the mass
\sigma_{|L^{\prime}|}^{*}
corresponding the location with the smallest payoff
p_{l}
in the set
L^{\prime}
to the location with the highest payoff
p_{l}
in the set
L^{\prime}
. In particular, we transfer
\min\{\sigma_{|L^{\prime}|}^{*},\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k}-\sigma_{1}^{*}\}
from location
|L^{\prime}|
to location
1
. If location
|L^{\prime}|
’s mass is exhausted before filling up location one to
\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k}
, then we transfer further mass of location
|L^{\prime}-1|
to location one. On the other hand, if location
1
is filled up to
\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k}
with location
|L^{\prime}|
, then the remaining mass from location
|L^{\prime}|
is transferred to location two in an analogous way. We then repeat this process of transferring mass from locations with lower payoffs
p_{l}
to those with higher payoffs
p_{l}
in the set
L^{\prime}
until there is at most one location remaining such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right)
, where
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
is the administrator strategy constructed by the above procedure.

Having constructed the strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
in the above manner, it is then straightforward to see that
P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\leq P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
as:

\displaystyle P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L^{\prime}}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in L\backslash L^{\prime}}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L^{\prime}}(1-\sigma_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in L\backslash L^{\prime}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L^{\prime}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in L\backslash L^{\prime}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L^{\prime}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in L\backslash L^{\prime}}(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})p_{l},

\displaystyle=P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}),

where (a) follows as
\sigma_{l}^{*}=\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}
for all
l\in L\backslash L^{\prime}
, (b) follows by our algorithmic procedure of constructing
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
as we transfer mass from locations with lower payoffs
p_{l}
to those with higher payoffs
p_{l}
in the set
L^{\prime}
, and (c) follows as
y_{l}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\leq 1=y_{l}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
for all
l\in L^{\prime}
.

The above analysis establishes our claim that there exists an administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
that is a solution to Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) such that for at most one location
l^{\prime}
, it holds that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right)
, which establishes our claim.

B.3
Proof of Theorem
2

We prove this result through a reduction from an instance of the partition problem. A partition instance consists of a sequence of numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
with
\sum_{l\in[n]}a_{l}=A
and involves the task of deciding whether there is some subset
S_{1}
of numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
. Without loss of generality, we consider a partition instance where
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2}
. Note that if
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}>\frac{A}{2}
, then clearly, there is no subset
S_{1}
of numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
, i.e., such an instance of partition can be solved in polynomial time.

We now construct an instance of the payoff maximization problem. In particular, we consider an instance with
n+1
locations, where the first
n
locations correspond to each number of the partition instance, where we define
p_{l}=a_{l}
and let
\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}=\frac{a_{l}}{A}
for all locations
l\in[n]
. Furthermore, we consider a location
n+1
such that
p_{n+1}=\max_{l\in[n]}p_{l}+\epsilon
and
\frac{d_{n+1}}{d_{n+1}+k}=\frac{1}{2}+\delta
, where
\epsilon>0
and
\delta>0
can be interpreted as small constants. We choose
\epsilon
such that
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}+\epsilon<A
, which is well defined as we consider partition instances where
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2}
. Finally, we let the total resource budget
R=\frac{1}{2}
. Given this instance of the payoff maximization problem, we now show that a sequence of numbers correspond to a “Yes” instance of the partition problem if and only if the objective of Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) for above defined instance is at most
\frac{A}{2}+v_{n+1}
, i.e., the total payoff is at least
\frac{A}{2}
.

(
\implies
:) We first suppose that we have a “Yes” instance of the partition problem, i.e., there exists a set
S
of numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
. In this case, we present a method to construct a feasible resource allocation strategy
\bm{\sigma}
of the administrator that achieves
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}
. In particular, we consider an allocation
\bm{\sigma}
for the above defined instance of the payoff maximization problem, where
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
units of resources are allocated to all locations
l\in S
and
\sigma_{l}=0
for all
l\in[n+1]\backslash S
. Such an allocation is feasible for this instance of the payoff maximization problem as:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n+1]}\sigma_{l}=\sum_{l\in S}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}=\sum_{l\in S}\frac{a_{l}}{A}=\frac{1}{A}\sum_{l\in S}a_{l}=\frac{1}{A}\times\frac{A}{2}=\frac{1}{2}=R,

i.e., the resource constraint is satisfied.

Furthermore, the objective of Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) under the strategy
\bm{\sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in[n+1]}(1-\sigma_{l})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S}(1-\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k})y_{l}(\bm{\sigma})p_{l}+\sum_{l\in[n+1]\backslash S}(1-0)y_{l}(\bm{\sigma})p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in[n]\backslash S}p_{l}+p_{n+1},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1},

where (a) follows as
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all
l\in S
and
\sigma_{l}=0
for all
l\in[n+1]\backslash S
, (b) follows as
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma})=0
when
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
and
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma})=1
when
\sigma_{l}=0
by the best-response of users in Equation (
5
), and (c) follows as
\sum_{l\in[n]\backslash S}p_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
. The above relation implies that the payoff of the allocation
\bm{\sigma}
is given by

\displaystyle P_{R}(\bm{\sigma})=\sum_{l\in[n+1]}p_{l}-P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma})=A+p_{n+1}-\left(\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1}\right)=\frac{A}{2}.

The above relation implies that
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}
, and thus we have shown that a “Yes” instance of the partition problem implies that the administrator’s optimal payoff for the above defined instance is at least
\frac{A}{2}
, which establishes the forward direction of our claim.

(
\impliedby
:) Next, suppose that the optimal resource allocation strategy
\bm{\sigma}
of the administrator is such that
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}
, i.e.,
P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma})\leq\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1}
where
\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}
satisfies
\sum_{l\in[n+1]}\sigma_{l}\leq R=\frac{1}{2}
. From the structure of the optimal administrator strategy established in Proposition
1
, we note that the objective of Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) is given by:

\displaystyle P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma})=\sum_{l\in L_{2}}p_{l}+(1-\sigma_{l^{\prime}})p_{l^{\prime}},

(14)

for some location
l^{\prime}
and a set of locations
L_{2}
, where it holds that
\sum_{l\in[n+1]\backslash L_{2}}\sigma_{l}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. Then, we show that we have a “Yes” instance of the partition problem by considering two cases: (i)
l^{\prime}=\emptyset
and (ii)
l^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
.

Case (i):
In the setting when
l^{\prime}=\emptyset
, without loss of generality, for ease of notation, we define
L_{2}\cup\{n+1\}
as the set of locations for which
\sigma_{l}=0
and
L_{1}
as the set of locations for which
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
. Note here that as
R=\frac{1}{2}<\frac{1}{2}+\delta=\frac{d_{n+1}}{d_{n+1}+k}
it follows for location
n+1
that
\sigma_{n+1}=0
as
l^{\prime}=\emptyset
. Next, for our payoff maximization instance, we note by assumption that the following two inequalities are satisfied: (i)
\sum_{l\in L_{2}\cup\{n+1\}}p_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1}
and (ii)
\sum_{l\in L_{1}}\frac{a_{l}}{A}=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\leq\frac{1}{2}
. From the first inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}=\sum_{l\in L}p_{l}-\sum_{l\in L_{2}\cup\{n+1\}}p_{l}\geq\frac{A}{2},

where the inequality follows as
\sum_{l\in[n+1]}a_{l}=A+p_{n+1}
and the fact that
\sum_{l\in L_{2}\cup\{n+1\}}p_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1}
. Combining this derived inequality with the resource constraint that
\sum_{l\in L_{1}}\frac{a_{l}}{A}\leq\frac{1}{2}
, it follows that
\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
, i.e., we have a “Yes” instance of the partition problem.

Case (ii):
In the setting when
l^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, we first show that it must follow that
l^{\prime}=\{n+1\}
. At a high-level, this result follows as location
n+1
has the highest payoff of
p_{l}
among all the locations. We now proceed by contradiction to establish this claim by supposing that
l^{\prime}\neq\{n+1\}
. Next, note that by our constructed instance that
\frac{d_{n+1}}{d_{n+1}+k}=\frac{1}{2}+\delta>R
and thus the location
\{n+1\}\in L_{2}
.

To derive our contradiction, we consider an allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
where
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}=0
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{n+1}=\sigma_{l^{\prime}}
. Note that
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{l^{{}^{\prime}}}}{d_{l^{{}^{\prime}}}+k}\right)
and thus it also holds that
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}\in\left(0,\frac{d_{n+1}}{d_{n+1}+k}\right)
, as
\frac{d_{n+1}}{d_{n+1}+k}>\frac{1}{2}\geq\frac{\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}}{A}=\frac{d_{l^{{}^{\prime}}}}{d_{l^{{}^{\prime}}}+k}
. Clearly the new allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
is feasible as
\bm{\sigma}
is feasible. Next, we observe that

\displaystyle P_{R}^{-}(\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L_{2}}p_{l}+(1-\sigma_{l^{\prime}})p_{l^{\prime}}=\sum_{l\in L_{2}\backslash\{n+1\}}p_{l}+p_{n+1}+(1-\sigma_{l^{\prime}})p_{l^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{>}}\sum_{l\in L_{2}\cup\{l^{\prime}\}}p_{l}+(1-\sigma_{l^{\prime}})p_{n+1},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L_{2}\cup\{l^{\prime}\}}p_{l}+(1-\tilde{\sigma}_{n+1})p_{n+1},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}),

where (a) follows from Equation (
14
), (b) follows as
p_{n+1}=\max_{l\in[n]}v_{l}+\epsilon>p_{l^{\prime}}
, (c) follows as
\tilde{\sigma}_{n+1}=\sigma_{l^{\prime}}
, and (d) follows by the definition of
P_{R}^{-}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})
and the fact that the only change in the allocation between
\bm{\sigma}
and
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
is for locations
l^{\prime}
and
n+1
. The above relations imply that the allocation strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
achieves a lower objective for Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) than the strategy
\bm{\sigma}
, a contradiction. Thus, it follows that if
l^{\prime}\neq\emptyset
, then it must be that
l^{\prime}=\{n+1\}
for our above defined instance of the payoff maximization problem.

Next, recall that
L_{2}
are the set of locations for which
\sigma_{l}=0
and
L_{1}
are the set of locations for which
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
. Then, for our payoff maximization instance, we have that the following two inequalities hold:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{2}}p_{l}+(1-\sigma_{n+1})p_{n+1}\leq\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1},

(15)

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{2}}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}+\sigma_{n+1}\leq\frac{1}{2}.

(16)

The first inequality represents the fact that the optimal objective of the Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) is at most
\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1}
and the second inequality implies that the total resource spending does not exceed the available resources.

Then, noting that
\sum_{l\in[n+1]}p_{l}=A+p_{n+1}
, we have from the Equation (
15
) that:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\sigma_{n+1}p_{n+1}=\sum_{l\in[n+1]}p_{l}-\left(\sum_{l\in L_{2}}p_{l}+(1-\sigma_{n+1})p_{n+1}\right)\geq A+p_{n+1}-\left(\frac{A}{2}+p_{n+1}\right)=\frac{A}{2}.

Substituting
p_{l}=a_{l}
and
p_{n+1}=\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}+\epsilon
in the above inequality, we obtain that:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}+\sigma_{n+1}(\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}+\epsilon)\geq\frac{A}{2}.

(17)

Moreover, substituting
\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}=\frac{a_{l}}{A}
in Equation (
16
), we obtain that:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}+\sigma_{n+1}A\leq\frac{A}{2}.

(18)

Combining Equations (
17
) and (
18
), we obtain that:

\displaystyle\sigma_{n+1}(\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}+\epsilon)\geq\sigma_{n+1}A.

Note that this inequality can only be satisfied if either
\sigma_{n+1}=0
or
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}+\epsilon\geq A=\sum_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
. Note that the latter inequality cannot be satisfied as
\epsilon
has been chosen to be a small constant such that
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}+\epsilon<A
, where, recall that
\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2}
. As a result, the above relation implies
\sigma_{n+1}=0
, which, from Equations (
17
) and (
18
), implies the following two relations:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}\geq\frac{A}{2}\text{ and }\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2},

which together imply that
\sum_{l\in L_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
. Thus, again, we have a “Yes” instance of partition, which establishes our claim.

B.4
Proof of Theorem
3

B.4.1
Proof Overview

We first define a linear program resembling a fractional knapsack like optimization and leverage Proposition
1
to show that the optimal administrator payoff is upper bounded by the optimal objective of this linear program. Next, since the optimal objective of the fractional knapsack problem satisfies
\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+x_{\tilde{l}}p_{\tilde{l}}
for some subset of locations
S
and a location
\tilde{l}
with
x_{\tilde{l}}\leq\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}}{d_{\tilde{l}}+k}
, to establish the desired half approximation, we show that (i)
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq x_{\tilde{l}}p_{\tilde{l}}
and (ii)
P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})\geq\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}
, where the allocations
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
are as defined in Algorithm
2
. Notice that the proof of claim (i) is by construction as
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
by definition is chosen to maximize the payoff from spending on a single location. The proof of claim (ii) relies on the fact that the greedy algorithm in Step 1 on Algorithm
2
precisely corresponds to the greedy algorithm to optimize the knapsack linear program. Finally, combining the results of claims (i) and (ii), the desired result follows.

B.4.2
Complete Proof of Theorem
3

To prove this claim, we first recall from Proposition
1
that the payoff maximizing objective of the administrator at the solution
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
is given by
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}p_{\tilde{l}}
for some subset of locations
L_{1}
where
\sigma_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all
l\in L_{1}
and a location
\tilde{l}
such that
\sum_{l\in L_{1}}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}+\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}\leq R
. Furthermore, we consider the following knapsack optimization problem:

\displaystyle G_{R}^{\prime}=\max_{0\leq x_{l}\leq 1}\sum_{l\in L}x_{l}p_{l}\text{ s.t. }\sum_{l\in L}t_{l}x_{l}\leq R.

(19)

Then, to prove this claim, we proceed in two steps. First, we show that the optimal payoff-maximizing objective of the administrator is no more than
G_{R}^{\prime}
, the optimal objective corresponding to Problem (
19
). Then, we use this result and the property of the optimal payoff-maximizing objective of the administrator from Proposition
1
to establish the half approximation guarantee of Algorithm
2
.

Proof of
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq G_{R}^{\prime}
:

To prove this claim, first note by the structure of the optimal solution established in Proposition
1
that
\sigma^{*}_{l}\geq 0
and
\sigma^{*}_{l}\leq\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all locations
l
, and by the feasibility of
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
that
\sum_{l\in L}\sigma^{*}_{l}\leq R
. Then, we transform
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
to a feasible solution of Problem (
19
) by defining
x_{l}=\frac{1}{t_{l}}\sigma_{l}^{*}
for all
l
. Note that
\mathbf{x}=(x_{l})_{l\in L}
is a feasible solution to Problem (
19
) as, by definition of
\mathbf{x}
it holds that
0\leq x_{l}\leq 1
, and the resource constraint is satisfied:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L}t_{l}x_{l}=\sum_{l\in L}t_{l}\frac{1}{t_{l}}\sigma_{l}^{*}=\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq R.

Next, to show that
G_{R}^{\prime}\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
, we have:

\displaystyle G_{R}^{\prime}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L}x_{l}p_{l}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L_{1}}\frac{1}{t_{l}}\sigma_{l}^{*}p_{l}+\frac{1}{t_{\tilde{l}}}\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}p_{\tilde{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}p_{\tilde{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{=}}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}),

where (a) follows from the fact that
\mathbf{x}=(x_{l})_{l\in L}
is one of the feasible solutions to Problem (
19
), (b) follows from the fact that
x_{l}=\frac{1}{t_{l}}\sigma^{*}_{l}
and that
\sigma^{*}_{l}=0
for
l\notin L_{1}\cup\{\tilde{l}\}
, (c) follows from the fact that
\sigma_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}=t_{l}
for all
l\in L_{1}
and that
\frac{1}{t_{\tilde{l}}}\geq 1
, and (d) follows by the definition of
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
. Thus, we have shown that
G_{R}^{\prime}\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
.

Proving the Approximation Ratio:

Finally, to complete the proof of our claim, notice that as Problem (
19
) is a fractional knapsack problem that
G_{R}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}
for some subset
S
and allocation
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, where resources are allocated to locations in the descending orders of the affordable bang-per-buck ratios. Next, note by Algorithm
2
that

\displaystyle P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A})\geq P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})\geq\sum_{l\in S}p_{l},

(20)

where recall that
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
is the allocation corresponding to the greedy algorithm in Step 1 of Algorithm
2
and the second inequality follows as the greedy algorithm selects at least the first
S
locations in the descending order of their affordable bang-per-buck values. Furthermore, by Step 2 of Algorithm
2
and the construction of the allocation
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A})\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}.

(21)

Then, summing Equations (
20
) and (
21
), we obtain that:

\displaystyle 2P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A})\geq\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}=G_{R}^{\prime}\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}),

which establishes our claim that
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A})\geq\frac{1}{2}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
, i.e., Algorithm
2
is a half approximation to the optimal solution to the bi-level Program (
3a
)-(
3b
).

B.5
Proof of Theorem
4

To prove this result, we again consider the fractional knapsack Problem (
19
) defined in the proof of Theorem
3
and use the fact that the objective of the fractional knapsack Problem (
19
) upper bounds the optimal administrator payoff, given by
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
. In particular, to establish the desired resource augmentation guarantee, we first recall from the optimal solution of the fractional knapsack problem that
G_{R}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}
for some subset
S
and allocation
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, where resources are allocated to locations in the descending orders of their affordable bang-per-buck ratios. Next, given
R+1
resources, we define
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1}
as the allocation corresponding to Step 1 of Algorithm
2
and let
\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{R+1}
be the allocation corresponding to Algorithm
2
with
R+1
resources. We now show that
P_{R+1}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{R+1})\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
to prove our claim.

To show this, we note by our greedy procedure in Step 1 of Algorithm
2
that
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1}\geq\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
and that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{R+1}=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}
. Such a result holds as the additional resource ensures that the administrator will allocate
\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\leq 1
units of resources to location
l^{\prime}
, as location
l^{\prime}
has the highest affordable bang-per-buck ratio among the remaining locations by definition (as
G_{R}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}
for some subset
S
and allocation
\mathbf{x}^{*}
, where resources are allocated to locations in the descending orders of the bang-per-buck). Since
\tilde{\sigma}^{R+1}_{l^{\prime}}=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}
, note by the best-response Problem (
5
) of users that
y_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})=0
. Consequently, as
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1}
corresponds to an allocation where resources are allocated to locations in the descending order of the affordable bang-per-buck ratios and, in particular, corresponds to an allocation where
\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\leq 1
fraction of resources are allocated to location
l^{\prime}
, it follows that

\displaystyle P_{R+1}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{R+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+p_{l^{\prime}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in S}p_{l}+x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}=G_{R}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\geq}}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})

where (a) follows from the fact that
\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{R+1}
corresponds to the outcome with a greater payoff from steps 1 and 2 of Algorithm
2
, (b) follows as
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1}
corresponds to an allocation of resources in the descending orders of the affordable bang-per-buck ratios when an administrator has an additional resource that it can spend on location
l^{\prime}
and that
y_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})=0
. Furthermore, (c) follows from the fact that
x_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k})
and (d) follows from our relation derived in proof of Theorem
3
that the optimal objective of the fractional knapsack Problem (
19
) is at least the payoff-maximizing objective. Thus, we have shown that
P_{R+1}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{R+1})\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
, which establishes our claim.

B.6
Proof of Theorem
5

To prove this claim, we first recall from Proposition
1
that the administrator’s optimal payoff is given by:
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}
for some set of locations
L_{1}
for which
\sigma_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all
l\in L_{1}
and at most one location
l^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}=R-\sum_{l\in L_{1}}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\leq\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}
and
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}
. We proceed by analysing two cases: (i)
|L_{1}|\leq m
and (ii)
|L_{1}|>m
.

Case (i) -
|L_{1}|\leq m
:

In this case, observe that given
\delta
additional resources there is one feasible pair
(S,\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta})
, where
S=L_{1}
and
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta}=\min\{\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}+\delta,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\}
, which is one of the pairs considered in the brute-force step of Algorithm
3
. Let
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
be allocation corresponding to applying the greedy procedure in step two of Algorithm
3
that extends the allocation corresponding to this pair
(S,\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta})
. Then, the payoff of the allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
satisfies

\displaystyle P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})\geq\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\min\left\{\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}+\delta,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\right\}p_{l^{\prime}}\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}),

where the first inequality follows as the payoff corresponding to
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
is at least that of the allocation to the pair
(S,\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta})
and the final inequality follows as
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}
. Finally, noting that the allocation
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
corresponds to the maximum payoff of Algorithm
3
across all feasible pairs, it follows that
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
, which establishes our claim for
|L_{1}|\leq m
.

Case (ii) -
|L_{1}|>m
:

In the case when
|L_{1}|\geq m+1
, consider a set
(S,\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta})
as follows. In particular, let
S=\{l_{1},\ldots,l_{m}\}
be the
m
locations under the optimal allocation with
\sigma_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
corresponding to the
m
highest payoffs, i.e., for each
l\in S
and
\tilde{l}\in L_{1}\backslash S
it holds that
p_{l}\geq p_{\tilde{l}}
. Moreover, let
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta}=\min\{\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}+\delta,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k}\}
be the allocation to location
l^{\prime}
. Then, observe that since all feasible pairs are considered in the brute-force step of Algorithm
3
,
(S,\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta})
corresponds to one such pair. Let
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
be allocation corresponding to applying the greedy procedure in step two of Algorithm
3
that extends the allocation corresponding to this pair
(S,\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\delta})
to the remaining location. We will now show that the allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
achieves the desired approximation guarantee.

To establish the desired approximation guarantee for the allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
, we first define
L_{2}=\{l_{m+1},\ldots,l_{x}\}
to be the location set with
\sigma_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
for all
l\in L_{1}\backslash S
under the optimal allocation, where the locations are ordered in descending order of their affordable bang-per-buck ratios. Furthermore, let
\bar{l}
be the first location in the set
L_{2}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{\bar{l}}<\sigma_{\bar{l}}^{*}
and let
\tilde{\sigma}^{G}
denote the total number of resources that are not allocated to any location by Algorithm
3
. Then, consider the set of locations
S_{1}
that are allocated resources under the greedy procedure in step two of Algorithm
3
with at least the bang-per-buck of location
\bar{l}
but not including location
\bar{l}
and let
S_{2}
be the set of locations allocated resources by the greedy procedure in step two of Algorithm
3
with a strictly lower bang-per-buck ratio than
\bar{l}
and includes the location
\bar{l}
. Then, by construction of the greedy procedure in step two of Algorithm
3
and the fact that
\tilde{\sigma}_{\bar{l}}<\sigma_{\bar{l}}^{*}
, note that
\sum_{l\in S_{2}}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}+\tilde{\sigma}^{G}<\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}
. Note that if this inequality were not true then the greedy procedure in step two of Algorithm
3
would allocate at least
\sigma_{\bar{l}}^{*}=\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}
to location
\bar{l}
, a contradiction.

Next, given the above definitions of the sets
S_{1}
and
S_{2}
and the location
\bar{l}
, we obtain the following lower bound on the payoff of the allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
:

\displaystyle P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})\geq\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}+\sum_{l\in S_{2}}p_{l}.

(22)

Note further that the locations picked in the greedy step of Algorithm
3
in set
S_{1}
are such that they satisfy
\frac{p_{l}}{\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}}\geq\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}}
for all
l\in S_{1}
, as the greedy procedure allocates resources to locations in the descending order of their bang-per-buck ratios. Consequently, it holds that:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}\geq\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}}\left(R+\delta-\tilde{\sigma}^{G}-\sum_{l\in S_{2}}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}-\sum_{l=1}^{{\bar{l}}-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}-\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}\right).

(23)

Then, from Equations (
22
) and (
23
), we obtain the following upper bound on the optimal payoff:

\displaystyle P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L_{1}}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}=\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\sum_{l=\bar{l}}^{x}p_{l},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\left(R-\sum_{l=1}^{\bar{l}-1}\sigma_{l}^{*}-\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\right)\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}-\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}+\left(R-\sum_{l=1}^{\bar{l}-1}\sigma_{l}^{*}-\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\right)\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}-\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}}\left(R+\delta-\tilde{\sigma}^{G}-\sum_{l\in S_{2}}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}-\sum_{l=1}^{\bar{l}-1}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}-\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}\right)

\displaystyle+\left(R-\sum_{l=1}^{\bar{l}-1}\sigma_{l}^{*}-\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\right)\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(d)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}+\frac{p_{\bar{l}}}{\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}}\left(\tilde{\sigma}^{G}+\sum_{l\in S_{2}}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}\right),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(e)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{l=1}^{m}p_{l}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}p_{l^{\prime}}+\sum_{l=m+1}^{\bar{l}-1}p_{l}+\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}+p_{\bar{l}},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(f)}}{{\leq}}P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})+p_{\bar{l}},

where (a) follows from the fact that all locations
\{l_{\bar{l}},\ldots,l_{x}\}
have at most the bang-per-buck ratio as location
{\bar{l}}
, (b) follows from adding an subtracting the term
\sum_{l\in S_{1}}p_{l}
, (c) follows from Equation (
23
), (d) follows from the fact that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}=\sigma_{l}^{*}
for all
l\in[\bar{l}-1]
for our selected subset in the brute-force step and the definition of
\bar{l}
along with the fact that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}\in[\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*},\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}+\delta]
. Moreover, (e) follows from the fact that
\tilde{\sigma}^{G}+\sum_{l\in S_{2}}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}<\frac{d_{\bar{l}}}{d_{\bar{l}}+k}
from our earlier analysis and (f) follows from Equation (
22
).

Then, observe that
p_{\bar{l}}\leq\frac{P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})}{m+1}
, as the first
m
locations in the set
S
all have a higher payoff than
p_{\bar{l}}
. Consequently, rearranging the above derived inequality
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})+p_{\bar{l}}
, it follows that
\left(1-\frac{1}{m+1}\right)P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})
. Finally, noting that the allocation
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
corresponds to the maximum payoff of Algorithm
3
across all feasible pairs, it follows that
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})
. Consequently, the obtained approximation guarantee for the allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
given by
\left(1-\frac{1}{m+1}\right)P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})
implies our desired approximation guarantee that
\left(1-\frac{1}{m+1}\right)P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})
, which establishes our claim.

B.7
Proof of Theorem
6

We prove Theorem
6
through a reduction from an instance of the partition problem. Recall that a partition instance consists of a sequence of numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
with
\sum_{l\in[n]}a_{l}=A
and involves the task of deciding whether there is some subset
S_{1}
of numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
.

In the following, we construct an instance of the heterogeneous revenue maximization problem (HRMP), henceforth referred to as HRMP for brevity, based on a partition instance and present four intermediate lemmas to complete the proof of Theorem
6
. We then present the detailed proofs of these lemmas in later subsections.

To this end, we begin by constructing an instance of the HRMP problem with with two types, i.e., where
|\mathcal{I}|=2
, and
n
locations, where each number
a_{l}
corresponds to a given location. In this setting, for ease of exposition, we drop the fine
k
from the revenue maximization Objective (
2a
) of the administrator as this is a uniform constant that applies to all locations
l
and types
i
. Then, we define the following four quantities: (i)
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}
, (ii)
\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}=\frac{a_{l}}{A}
, (iii)
\Lambda_{l}^{1}=A\left(2\frac{\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}}{a_{l}}-1\right)
, and (iv)
\Lambda_{l}^{2}=A\left(1+\frac{2\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}}{a_{l}}\right)
for all
l\in[n]
. Moreover, we define the resource budget as
R=\frac{3}{4}
.

Given this instance of HRMP, we claim that we have a “Yes” instance of partition if and only if there is a feasible allocation of resources to locations with total revenue that is at least
\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
for the above defined HRMP instance.

To establish this claim, we first prove its forward direction that if we have a “Yes” instance of partition, then there exists a feasible allocation of resources to locations with total revenue that is at least
\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
for the above defined HRMP instance.

Lemma 5

(Forward Direction of NP-Hardness of HRMP)
.

Consider a partition instance with a sequence of numbers
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
with
\sum_{l\in[n]}a_{l}=A
, such that there exists a subset
S_{1}
of numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
. Then, there exists a feasible allocation of resources to locations with total expected revenue that is at least
\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
for the above defined HRMP instance.

To prove this claim, we present a method to construct a feasible administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}
for the above defined HRMP instance and show that the revenue of this administrator strategy satisfies
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
. For a proof of Lemma
5
, see Appendix
B.7.1
.

Next, to prove the reverse direction of this claim, we suppose that the optimal resource allocation strategy corresponding to the above defined HRMP instance is such that the revenue
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
and the total resources
\sum_{l\in[n]}\sigma_{l}\leq\frac{3}{4}=R
. To prove that such a setting corresponds to a “Yes” instance of partition, we use three intermediate lemmas. Our first two lemmas establish certain structural properties of the administrator’s revenue maximizing solution. In particular, we first establish a lower bound on the resources allocated to each location under the revenue maximizing strategy of the administrator, as is elucidated through the following lemma.

Lemma 6

(Lower Bound on Administrator Allocation Strategy)
.

Suppose that there is some feasible administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}
that satisfies
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
for
R=\frac{3}{4}
. Then, there exists a revenue maximizing administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
for the above defined HRMP instance such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\geq\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for all locations
l
.

For a proof of Lemma
6
, see Appendix
B.7.2
. Given the result obtained in Lemma
6
, for the remainder of this proof, we focus on revenue maximizing administrator strategies
\bm{\sigma}
that satisfy
\sigma_{l}\geq\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for all locations
l
for our above defined HRMP instance.

Next, following a similar analysis in the proof of Proposition
1
, we can establish the following property on the optimal solution of Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
).

Lemma 7

(Structure of Revenue-Maximizing Solution)
.

Suppose that users at each location best-respond based on Equation (
6
). Then, there exists a revenue maximizing strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
of the administrator corresponding to the solution of Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
) such that:

•

There exists a set of locations
L_{1}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=\frac{d_{l}^{i_{l}}}{d_{l}^{i_{l}}+k}
for all
l\in L_{1}
for some type
i_{l}\in\mathcal{I}
that can be location specific, a set of locations
L_{2}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}=0
for all
l\in L_{2}
, and at most one location
l^{\prime}
such that either
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in(0,\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}+k})
or
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\in(\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{i-1}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{i-1}+k},\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{i}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{i}+k})
for some
i>1
. Here,
L_{1}
,
L_{2}
, and
l^{\prime}
are all disjoint and
L_{1}\cup L_{2}\cup\{l^{\prime}\}=L
.

Since the analysis of the above result follows similar arguments to that in the proof of Proposition
1
, we omit it for brevity.

Finally, we use the structure of the optimal (revenue maximizing) solution of the above defined HRMP instance established in Lemmas
6
and
7
to show that our HRMP instance with
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
corresponds to a “Yes” instance of partition.

Lemma 8

(Reverse Direction of NP-Hardness of HRMP)
.

Suppose that there is some feasible administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}
that satisfies
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
for
R=\frac{3}{4}
. Then, for the above defined HRMP instance, there exists a subset
S_{1}
of numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S_{1}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
, i.e., we have a “Yes” instance of partition.

For a proof of Lemma
8
, see Appendix
B.7.3
. Note that Lemmas
5
-
8
prove Theorem
6
.

B.7.1
Proof of Lemma
5

We first suppose that we have a “Yes” instance of partition, i.e., there exists a set of
S
numbers such that
\sum_{l\in S}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
. Then, consider an administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}
such that
\sigma_{l}=\frac{a_{l}}{A}
for all
l\in S
and
\sigma_{l}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}
for all
l\in[n]\backslash S
. We first note that
\bm{\sigma}
is feasible, i.e.,
\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}
, as
\sigma_{l}\in[0,1]
by construction and the resource constraint is satisfied as:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in[n]}\sigma_{l}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}+\sum_{l\in S\backslash[n]}\sigma_{l}=\sum_{l\in S}\frac{a_{l}}{A}+\sum_{l\in[n]\backslash S}\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{A}\frac{A}{2}+\frac{1}{2A}\frac{A}{2}=\frac{3}{4}=R,

where (a) follows as
\sum_{l\in S}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
and
\sum_{l\in[n]\backslash S}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
as we have a “Yes” instance of partition.

Next, we obtain the following revenue objective for the administrator when it plays the above defined strategy
\bm{\sigma}
:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}y_{l}^{i}(\bm{\sigma})\Lambda_{l}^{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S}\Lambda_{l}^{2}\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}+\sum_{l\in[n]\backslash S}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S}A\left(1\!+\!\frac{2\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}}{a_{l}}\right)\!\!\frac{a_{l}}{A}+\!\!\sum_{l\in S\backslash[n]}\!\!\!\left(A\left(2\frac{\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}}{a_{l}}\!-\!1\right)\!+\!A\left(1\!+\!\frac{2\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}}{a_{l}}\right)\right)\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A},

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in S}\left(a_{l}+2\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}\right)+\sum_{l\in S\backslash[n]}2\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}},

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{2}+2\sum_{l\in[n]}\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}},

\displaystyle=\frac{A}{2}+2n\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}},

where we have dropped the fine
k
from the objective for ease of exposition, (a) follows by the definition of
y_{l}^{i}(\bm{\sigma})
as given in Equation (
6
), and (b) follows by plugging in the values of the respective quantities in the above defined instance. The above analysis implies that
\bm{\sigma}
is both feasible and achieves a total revenue of
\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
, which establishes the forward direction of our claim.

B.7.2
Proof of Lemma
6

To prove this claim, we first show that in the setting when
R=\frac{3}{4}
any optimal administrator strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
satisfies
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\geq\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for all locations
l
.
To this end, suppose for contradiction that there is an optimal solution
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
to the HRMP instance such that there is some set of locations
S^{\prime}
for which
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}+k}
for all
l^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
. Furthermore, define
\gamma=\sum_{l\in L}\min\{\sigma_{l}^{*},\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}\}
to represent the total spending across locations to cover the first type
i=1
. In this case we note that
\gamma<\frac{1}{2}
, as

\displaystyle\gamma

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}\min\{\sigma_{l}^{*},\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}\}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{<}}\sum_{l\in L}\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in L}\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2},

where (a) follows as there is some set of location
S^{\prime}
for which
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}+k}
for all
l^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
by our contradiction assumption, (b) follows by substituting
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}
for our defined HRMP instance, and (c) follows as
\sum_{l\in[n]}a_{l}=A
.

We now define an allocation strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
with a strictly higher revenue than
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
to derive our desired contradiction. To do so, we first define
\delta=\sum_{l\in L}\max\{0,\sigma_{l}^{*}-\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}\}
as the cumulative resources allocated to locations beyond
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
. In this case, we note that
\delta\geq\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
, as if this were not the case then
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
would not be optimal (as the amount of resources
\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
could be spent on locations in the set
S^{\prime}
for which
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}+k}
for all
l^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
to obtain a higher revenue for the administrator). Then, we construct
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
by transferring
\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
of mass from locations
l\in[n]\backslash S^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{l}^{*}>\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
to locations in the set
S^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{l}^{*}<\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
, such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}\geq\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for all locations
l
and the total resource spending
\sum_{l\in L}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}=\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}
.

We now show that such a defined strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
achieves a strictly higher revenue than
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
. To see this, first note that the maximum additional gain in the revenue from spending
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}
as compared to
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
is given by
\Lambda_{l}^{2}\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}-(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}=a_{l}
for all locations
l
(see left of Figure
2
). Furthermore, the spending required to gain this amount
a_{l}
is given by
\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}=\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}
. In other words, for the additional spending of
\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
on locations in the set
[n]\backslash S^{\prime}
, the maximum gain in the administrator’s revenue is given by
(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma)\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
, regardless of which location(s) this additional amount
\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
is spent on. However, spending the same amount on locations in the set
S^{\prime}
with
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}{d_{l^{\prime}}^{1}+k}
for all
l^{\prime}\in S^{\prime}
, will result in an increase in the administrator revenue by

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime}}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})(\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}-\sigma_{l}^{*})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime}}4\frac{A}{a_{l}}\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}(\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}-\sigma_{l}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}4\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime}}(\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}-\sigma_{l}^{*}),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}4\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma)>(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma)\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l},

where (a) follows by substituting the relations for
\Lambda_{l}^{1}
and
\Lambda_{l}^{2}
for our above defined HRMP instance, (b) follows
A\geq a_{l}
for all locations
l
as
\sum_{l}a_{l}=A
, and (c) follows as
\sum_{l\in S^{\prime}}(\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}-\sigma_{l}^{*})=\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
by our definition of
\gamma
. Thus, we have obtained that the increase in the administrator’s revenue corresponding to shifting
\frac{1}{2}-\gamma
of resources from locations
l\in[n]\backslash S^{\prime}
to locations
l\in S^{\prime}
(as in our allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
) outweighs the maximum loss in the administrator objective from shifting these resources from
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
to
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
, given by
(\frac{1}{2}-\gamma)\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
. As a result, this relation implies that
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
is not optimal, a contradiction. Consequently, any optimal solution to the above defined HRMP instance must be such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\geq\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for all locations
l
, which establishes our claim.

B.7.3
Proof of Lemma
8

We now use the structure of the optimal solution established in Lemmas
6
and
7
to show that our HRMP instance with
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\times\max_{l\in[n]}a_{l}
corresponds to a “Yes” instance of partition. To do so, we first note that the total revenue when spending exactly
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
at each location
l
is given by:
\sum_{l\in[n]}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}=2n\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}
. Note that the total such spending on locations is
\sum_{l\in[n]}\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}=\frac{1}{2}
; hence, the total additional spending beyond
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
across all locations
l
must not be more than
\frac{1}{4}
as the total available resources
R=\frac{3}{4}
. In particular, we have that
\sum_{l\in[n]}x_{l}\leq\frac{1}{4}
, where
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}+x_{l}
for all locations
l
.

Next, from Lemma
7
, we have that there exists a solution to the HRMP problem such that there is at most one location
\tilde{l}
for which
x_{\tilde{l}}\in(0,\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k})=(0,\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{\tilde{l}}}{A})
. Such a result holds as from Lemma
6
we have that
\sigma_{l}\geq\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for all locations
l
and that from Lemma
7
, there exists at most one location
\tilde{l}
for which
\sigma_{l}\in(\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k},\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k})
. As a consequence, the spending of the administrator beyond
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
must satisfy
\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}+x_{\tilde{l}}\leq\frac{1}{4}
for some location set
S^{\prime\prime}
and some location
\tilde{l}
, and the revenue of the administrator must satisfy:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}\Lambda_{l}^{2}\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}\left(x_{\tilde{l}}+\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}\right)+\sum_{l\in[n]\backslash(S^{\prime\prime}\cup\{\tilde{l}\})}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}\Lambda_{l}^{2}\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}-\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}\left(x_{\tilde{l}}+\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}\right)

\displaystyle-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}(\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2})+\sum_{l\in[n]}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k},

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\left(x_{\tilde{l}}+\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}\right)\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}(\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2})+2n\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}},

where (a) follows from adding and subtracting
\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}\cup\tilde{l}}(\Lambda_{l}^{1}+\Lambda_{l}^{2})\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
, and (b) follows by substituting the relations for
\Lambda_{l}^{1}
,
\Lambda_{l}^{2}
, and
\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for our above defined HRMP instance.

Then, since
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq\frac{A}{2}+2n\max_{l^{\prime}\in[n]}a_{l^{\prime}}
, the above equalities implies that, we have the following relation is satisfied:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\left(x_{\tilde{l}}+\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}\right)\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}(\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2})\geq\frac{A}{2}.

(24)

We now show that we have a “Yes” instance of the partition problem by showing that
\tilde{l}=\emptyset
.

To show this, we proceed by contradiction and suppose that there is some location
\tilde{l}
such that
x_{\tilde{l}}\in(0,\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A})
. In this setting, we first note that the resource constraint
\frac{1}{4}\geq\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}\cup\{\tilde{l}\}}x_{l}=\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}+x_{\tilde{l}}
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}+x_{\tilde{l}}\leq\frac{1}{4},

\displaystyle\implies

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+2Ax_{\tilde{l}}\leq\frac{A}{2}

(25)

Furthermore, from Equation (
24
), we have that:

\displaystyle\frac{A}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\left(x_{\tilde{l}}+\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}\right)\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}(\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}),

\displaystyle<\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\frac{x_{\tilde{l}}}{\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}}a_{\tilde{l}},

(26)

where the second inequality follows as the total gain from spending
\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}
compared to
\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}
is given by
\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}(\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+\Lambda_{\tilde{l}}^{2})=a_{\tilde{l}}
and the fact that
\frac{x_{\tilde{l}}}{\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}}a_{\tilde{l}}
represents a strict upper bound on the expected revenue that the administrator can gain for any
x_{\tilde{l}}\in(0,\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k})
. For more intuition on the derivation of the second inequality, we refer to Figure
7
, which provides a geometric intuition for this claim through a depiction of the revenue function (in blue) and its corresponding upper bound (in orange).

Figure 7
:
Depiction of the revenue of the above defined HRMP instance with the amount of resources spent at location
\tilde{l}
in the range
\left[0,\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}\right]
. The curve in blue represents the revenue as a function of the number of resources allocated to location
\tilde{l}
, while the dashed line in orange represents the upper bound on the revenue function. Moreover, the two points marked in yellow represent the revenue (bottom yellow point) and its corresponding upper bound (top yellow point) corresponding to a resource allocation of
\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}+x_{\tilde{l}}
at location
\tilde{l}
.

From Equations (
25
) and (
26
), we obtain that:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\frac{x_{\tilde{l}}}{\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}}a_{\tilde{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\frac{x_{\tilde{l}}}{\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{\tilde{l}}}{A}}a_{\tilde{l}}=\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+2Ax_{\tilde{l}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\frac{A}{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{<}}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}+\frac{x_{\tilde{l}}}{\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}}a_{\tilde{l}},

for any
x_{\tilde{l}}\in(0,\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A})
, a contradiction. Here, (a) follows by substituting for the values of
\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{d_{l}^{2}+k}
and
\frac{d_{l}^{1}}{d_{l}^{1}+k}
for our above defined HRMP instance, (b) follows by Equation (
25
), and (c) follows by Equation (
26
). Thus, we have that
\tilde{l}\neq\emptyset
is not possible.

Finally, since
\tilde{l}=\emptyset
, we have that Equation (
24
) reduces to:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}\geq\frac{A}{2}.

(27)

Moreover, we have by the resource constraint that for these locations
S^{\prime\prime}
that
\frac{1}{4}\geq\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}x_{l}=\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{2}+k}-\frac{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}}{d_{\tilde{l}}^{1}+k}=\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}\frac{1}{2}\frac{a_{l}}{A}
. This inequality for the resource constraint implies the following relation:

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}\leq\frac{A}{2}.

(28)

Together, Equations (
27
) and (
28
) imply that there exists a set
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
\sum_{l\in S^{\prime\prime}}a_{l}=\frac{A}{2}
, i.e., we have a “Yes” instance of partition. This establishes our claim.

B.8
Proof of Theorem
7

To prove this claim, we begin by first introducing some notation. In particular, let
Q_{l}(\cdot)
denote the revenue function at each location as a function of the amount of allocated resources
\sigma_{l}
to that location (e.g., see left of Figure
2
). Moreover, we denote
\hat{Q}_{l}(\cdot)
as the MCUA of the revenue function at each location
l
. We then have the following problem of maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\bm{\sigma}}{\mathrm{max}}\quad\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}),

(29a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\bm{\sigma}}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}\leq R,

(29b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\in[0,1],\quad\forall l\in L,

(29c)

which rather than maximizing the sum of the revenue functions across all locations corresponds to maximizing the sum of the MCUA of the revenue functions across locations subject to the constraint that
\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}
, as given by the Constraints (
29b
)-(
29c
).

Furthermore, for convenience of analysis, we introduce Algorithm
6
, which is analogous to the greedy allocation rule in step one of Algorithm
4
other than that the resources are allocated until either all the resources are exhausted or there are no further segments remaining to iterate over. Recall that step one of Algorithm
4
terminates when a segment’s resource requirement exceeds the amount of available resources.

Input :
Total Resource capacity
R
, Segment set
\mathcal{S}=\{(l_{s},c_{s},x_{s})\}_{s\in\mathcal{S}}
of MCUA of revenue function

Output :
Resource Allocation Strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}

Define affordability threshold
t_{l}\leftarrow\min\left\{R,\max_{i}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}\right\}
for all locations
l
;

Generate MCUA of the revenue function in range
[0,t_{l}]
for each location
l
;

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\leftarrow
Ordered list of segments
s
across all locations of this MCUA in descending order of slopes
c_{s}
;

Initialize available resources
\tilde{R}\leftarrow R
;

Initialize allocation strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}\leftarrow\mathbf{0}
;

for

segment
s\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}

do

\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s}}\leftarrow\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s}}+\min\{\tilde{R},x_{s}\}
;
Allocate the minimum of the available resources and
x_{s}
to location
l_{s}
;

\tilde{R}\leftarrow\tilde{R}-\min\{\tilde{R},x_{s}\}
;
Update amount of remaining resources
;

end for

Algorithm 6

Greedy Algorithm for MCUA of Revenue Function

We now establish Theorem
7
using three intermediate lemmas. Our first lemma shows that the allocation computed via Algorithm
6
optimizes the MCUA of the revenue function, as is elucidated by the following lemma.

Lemma 9

(MCUA is optimized by Algorithm
6
)
.

The allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
computed using Algorithm
6
is an optimal solution to the problem of maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function, i.e.,
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
is an optimal solution to Problem (
29a
)-(
29c
).

Our next lemma establishes a key structural property that the allocation corresponding to Algorithm
6
is such that the associated MCUA coincides with the original revenue function at all but at most one location.

Lemma 10

(Structure of Greedy Allocation)
.

The allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
computed via Algorithm
6
is such that it does not coincide with the original revenue function at at most one location, i.e., there is at most one location
l^{\prime}
such that
\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}})\neq Q_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}})
.

Our final lemma establishes that the MCUA of the revenue function coincides with the original revenue function at
\sigma_{l}=0
and at the allocation
\sigma_{l}=\sigma_{l}^{\max}
at which the revenue function is maximized.

Lemma 11

(MCUA Coincides with Revenue Function at Endpoints)
.

Let
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l})
be the MCUA of the revenue function
Q_{l}(\sigma_{l})
. Then, the MCUA of the revenue function coincides with the revenue function at the allocations
\sigma_{l}=0
and
\sigma_{l}=\sigma_{l}^{\max}
, i.e.,
\hat{Q}_{l}(0)=Q_{l}(0)
and
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})=Q_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})
.

Finally, we leverage Lemmas
9
,
10
, and
11
to establish Theorem
7
, as is elucidated by the following corollary.

Corollary 1

(1/2 Approximation for Heterogeneous Revenue Maximization)
.

Suppose
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
is the optimal enforcement strategy corresponding to the solution of Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
) and
\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*}
is the allocation corresponding to Algorithm
4
.
Then,
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*})\geq\frac{1}{2}Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
.

B.8.1
Proof of Lemma
9

To prove this claim, let
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
be the solution corresponding to Algorithm
6
and let
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
be the allocation that maximizes the MCUA of the revenue function. Furthermore, suppose for contradiction that Algorithm
6
is not optimal, i.e.,
\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l})<\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})
. Such a relation implies by the (strict) monotonicity of the MCUA of the revenue function at each location that there exists a location
l^{\prime}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}<\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{*}
. However, we note that the total amount of resources allocated by Algorithm
6
is given by
\min\left\{R,\sum_{l\in L}\max_{i}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}\right\}
. However, note that the optimal allocation also cannot allocate any more resources than this amount to guarantee feasibility, i.e., it must be the case that
\sum_{l\in L}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}=\sum_{l\in L}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}
, which implies that there exists some location
\tilde{l}
such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}>\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}
.

To derive our desired contradiction, we now construct another feasible strategy with a strictly higher objective than the strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
. In particular, consider
\epsilon>0
as some small positive constant such that
\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon\leq\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}\geq\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}+\epsilon
. Then, define the strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}=(\sigma_{l}^{\prime})_{l\in L}
, where
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}
for all
l\neq l^{\prime},\tilde{l}
,
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}=\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon
and
\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{\prime}=\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon
. Note that this new strategy is feasible. In particular, it is straightforward to check that
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}\in\left[0,\max_{i}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}\right]
for all locations
l
and that the resource constraint is satisfied as:

\displaystyle\sum_{l}\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\sigma_{l}^{\prime}+\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\prime}+\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{\prime}=\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}+\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon+\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon=\sum_{l}\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*}\leq R,

where the final inequality follows by the feasibility of
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
.

Next, we show that the MCUA Objective (
29a
) of this new strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
is greater than that of
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
. To see this, note that:

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}),

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})+\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon)+\hat{Q}_{\tilde{l}}(\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\neq\{l^{\prime},\tilde{l}\}}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})+\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})-\partial\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon)\epsilon+\hat{Q}_{\tilde{l}}(\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*})+\partial\hat{Q}_{\tilde{l}}(\sigma_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon)\epsilon,

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})+\epsilon(\partial\hat{Q}_{\tilde{l}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon)-\partial\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon)),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})+\epsilon(\partial\hat{Q}_{\tilde{l}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon)-\partial\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}})),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L}\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})=\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}),

where (a) follows by the concavity of the MCUA of the revenue function and the fact that the MCUA of the revenue function is super-differentiable at all points in the domain
\sigma_{l}\in[0,t_{l}]
. Note that any concave function on a non-empty convex set is super-differentiable at each interior point and the super-differentiability at the points
\sigma_{l}=0
and
\sigma_{l}=t_{l}
follow as an immediate consequence of the fact that an affine function upper bounds the welfare function due to the discreteness of the distribution. Next, inequality (b) follows by the fact that
\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}
is a monotonically increasing concave function and hence its derivative (and minimum sub-gradient) is monotonically non-increasing and (c) follows from the fact that the minimum sub-gradient
\partial\hat{Q}_{\tilde{l}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{\tilde{l}}^{*}+\epsilon)\geq\partial\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}))
by the nature of the greedy algorithm that allocates resources to locations in the descending order of the slopes of the segments of the MCUA of the revenue function (as otherwise the greedy algorithm would have allocated more resources to location
l^{\prime}
instead of location
\tilde{l}
). The above relationship implies that the allocation strategy
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
is optimal. We can then repeat the above procedure of comparing the allocation
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
to the greedy allocation corresponding to Algorithm
6
and construct a sequence of allocations that will eventually correspond exactly to the greedy allocation, which yields our desired contradiction. Consequently, Algorithm
6
maximizes the MCUA of the revenue function.

B.8.2
Proof of Lemma
10

Suppose for contradiction that there are at least two locations
l_{1},l_{2}
such that
\hat{Q}_{l_{1}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{1}})>Q_{l_{1}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{1}})
and
\hat{Q}_{l_{2}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{2}})>Q_{l_{2}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{2}})
. Note by the definition of the MCUA that the points at which the MCUA and the original revenue function do not coincide correspond to line segments with a fixed slope. Consequently, since the greedy algorithm, i.e., Algorithm
6
, allocates some resources to both line segments corresponding to locations
l_{1}
and
l_{2}
it follows that the slope of these segments must be the same by the nature of the greedy algorithm. But, under our greedy algorithm, we iterate through each of the segments sequentially, which implies that there is at most one segment
s
for which the allocation is strictly less than
x_{s}
.
Finally, noting that if a segment is allocated either zero or
x_{s}
that the resulting allocation lies on the original revenue function, it follows that there is at most one location for which
\hat{Q}_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l})>Q_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l})
, which establishes our claim.

B.8.3
Proof of Lemma
11

We first show that
\hat{Q}_{l}(0)=Q_{l}(0)
. To see this, we proceed by contradiction and assume that
\hat{Q}_{l}(0)>Q_{l}(0)
.

To establish our desired contradiction, we first note two properties of the MCUA of the revenue function. First note that at the points where the MCUA of the revenue function does not coincide with the revenue function, the associated MCUA is affine. Note that if the MCUA was not affine at the points where it does not coincide with the revenue function, then this would violate the definition of the MCUA, which is a point-wise smallest concave function that upper bounds the revenue function. Next, note by the definition of the MCUA of the revenue function that the MCUA must coincide with the revenue function at some point. Note that if the MCUA did not coincide with the MCUA at at least one point in our compact convex domain, we could point-wise decrease the associated function by a constant, which would violate the fact that the function was an MCUA of the revenue function.

Consequently, let
\sigma_{l}^{1}
be the first resource consumption value at which the MCUA of the revenue function coincides with the revenue function, i.e.,
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{1})=Q_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{1})
. Moreover, let
s_{1}
be the slope of the segment from
\hat{Q}_{l}(0)
to the first
\sigma_{l}
