at which
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l})=Q_{l}(\sigma_{l})
. Since
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l})>Q_{l}(\sigma_{l})
for all
\sigma_{l}\in[0,\sigma_{l}^{1})
, i.e., there exists some
\epsilon>0
such that
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l})+\epsilon\geq Q_{l}(\sigma_{l})
for all
\sigma_{l}\in[0,\sigma_{l}^{1})
, it follows that if we consider the segment from
\hat{Q}_{l}(0)-\epsilon
to
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{1})
, the corresponding curve is point-wise no greater than the original MCUA function and is strictly smaller over the domain
\sigma_{l}\in[0,\sigma_{l}^{1})
. Moreover, the resulting curve preserves concavity, as the new segment is a slope that is strictly greater than
s_{1}
. Consequently, we have constructed a new function that satisfies the required properties of an MCUA and is strictly smaller than the original MCUA function over the domain
\sigma_{l}\in[0,\sigma_{l}^{1})
. Thus, we have obtained our desired contradiction that the original function was an MCUA, which implies that
\hat{Q}_{l}(0)=Q_{l}(0)
.

We can apply an entirely analogous line of reasoning as above to establish that
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})=Q_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})
.

B.8.4
Proof of Corollary
1

To establish this claim, we first introduce some notation. In particular, let
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
be the solution corresponding to maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function. Furthermore, let
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
be the solution corresponding to maximizing the revenue function. Then, it follows that:

\displaystyle\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\hat{Q}_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}),

(30)

where (a) follows from the optimality of
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
for the problem of maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function and (b) follows from the fact that the MCUA of the revenue function is a point-wise upper bound on the revenue function at each location. Consequently, the above inequalities imply that the optimal objective of the MCUA of the revenue function is at least that of maximizing the revenue function directly.

In the remainder of this proof, we leverage Lemmas
9
,
10
, and
11
to show that the allocation corresponding to Algorithm
4
in fact upper bounds the optimal objective of the MCUA of the revenue function (up to a factor of two), which thus implies the desired half approximation guarantee by Equation (
30
). Thus, in the remainder of this proof we show that
2Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*})\geq\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
.

To establish this inequality, we first note from Lemmas
9
,
10
, and
11
that the greedy allocation corresponding to Algorithm
6
achieves an optimal MCUA objective of
\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})=\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}Q_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})+\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})
, with at most one location
l^{\prime}
such that
\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})>Q_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})
. We now establish the following two inequalities: (i)
Q_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})\geq\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}Q_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})
and (ii)
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})
, where
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}
and
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
are the allocations corresponding to steps one and two of Algorithm
4
, respectively.

Inequality (i):

We recall from earlier that the solution maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function exactly corresponds to the solution in step one of Algorithm
4
other than that resources are allocated until either all the resources are exhausted or there are no further segments remaining to iterate over. Consequently, it holds that
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l}=\tilde{\sigma}_{l}
for all
l\neq l^{\prime}
and for
l=l^{\prime}
it holds that
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l^{\prime}}\geq\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}
, where
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l^{\prime}}\leq\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}+x_{s^{\prime}}
. Thus, it follows that
Q_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}})=\sum_{l\in L\backslash\{l^{\prime}\}}Q_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})
, which implies our desired inequality.

Inequality (ii):

Furthermore, observe that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq Q_{l^{\prime}}(\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\max})
, where
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\max}
corresponds to the allocation that maximizes the revenue at location
l^{\prime}
among the set of all affordable allocations, i.e.,
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\max}=\argmax_{\sigma_{l^{\prime}}\in[0,t_{l}]}Q_{l^{\prime}}(\sigma_{l})
. Since the MCUA of the revenue function is such that
\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})=Q_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})
(see Lemma
11
) for all locations
l
and is monotonically increasing, it follows that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq Q_{l^{\prime}}(\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\max})=\hat{Q}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\max})\geq\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})
.

Together, inequalities (i) and (ii) imply that:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})+Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\geq\sum_{l\neq l^{\prime}}Q_{l}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{*})+\hat{Q}_{l^{\prime}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}}^{*})=\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}).

(31)

Finally, since
\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*}=\argmax\{Q_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}),Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\}
from step 3 of Algorithm
4
, it follows from the above
inequality that

\displaystyle 2Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*})\geq Q_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})+Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}),

where (a) follows by Equation (
31
) and (b) follows by Equation (
30
). Thus, we have obtained our desired half approximation guarantee, which establishes our desired result.

B.9
Proof of Theorem
8

To prove this claim, we first recall that, by construction, the MCUA is an upper bound on the revenue function for all feasible administrator strategies, i.e.,
\hat{Q}_{R}(\bm{\sigma})\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})
for all
\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}
(also see proof of Theorem
7
). Next, to establish our resource augmentation guarantee with
R+1
resources, we define
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1}
as the allocation corresponding to Step 1 of Algorithm
4
with
R+1
resources and
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1}
as the allocation corresponding to Algorithm
4
with
R+1
resources. Moreover, we let
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
be the optimal solution corresponding to directly optimizing the MCUA of the expected revenue function as given by Algorithm
6
. We now show that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1})
to establish our claim.

To show that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1})
, we establish four key relations: (i)
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
, (ii)
\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})
, (iii)
\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})=Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})
, and (iv)
Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})\leq Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1})
.

Relation (i):
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})
:

By construction, the MCUA of the revenue function upper bounds the revenue for all allocation strategies
\bm{\sigma}
. Hence, it follows that:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}),

where the first inequality follows as the MCUA of the revenue function upper bounds the revenue at
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
and the latter inequality follows by the optimality of
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*}
for the MCUA of the revenue function.

Relation (ii):
\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})
:

We recall from the proof of Theorem
7
that the allocation maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function corresponds to the solution of Algorithm
6
. In other words, the allocation maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function corresponds to the solution in the step 1 of Algorithm
4
, where resources are allocated until either all the resources are exhausted or there are no further segments are remaining to iterate over. Consequently, by the termination condition of step one of Algorithm
6
, there is at most one segment
s^{\prime}
and associated location
l_{s^{\prime}}
such that the solutions of step one of Algorithm
4
and that of Algorithm
6
differ. In particular, it holds that
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l}=\tilde{\sigma}_{l}
for all
l\neq l_{s^{\prime}}
and for
l=l_{s^{\prime}}
it holds that
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}\geq\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}
, where
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}\leq\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}+x_{s^{\prime}}
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}>\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}
, as if this were not the case, then our desired inequality trivially holds.

Next, with
R+1
resources, it must hold that the allocation
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1}
corresponding to step one of Algorithm
4
satisfies
\tilde{\sigma}_{l}^{R+1}\geq\tilde{\sigma}_{l}=\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l}
for all
l\neq l_{s^{\prime}}
, as the greedy allocation with
R+1
resources will at least allocate the same amount of resources to all locations as that with
R
resources. Furthermore, since
x_{s^{\prime}}\leq 1
for all segments
s
, it also holds for
l=l_{s^{\prime}}
that the greedy allocation will completely allocate
x_{s^{\prime}}
to location
l_{s^{\prime}}
; hence, it holds that
\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}^{R+1}\geq\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}+x_{s^{\prime}}\geq\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l_{s^{\prime}}}
.

Thus, we have established for all locations
l
that
\tilde{\sigma}^{R+1}_{l}\geq\tilde{\sigma}^{*}_{l}
. Finally, our desired inequality that
\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})
follows by the monotonicity of the MCUA of the revenue function in the resource spending at each location.

Relation (iii):
\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})=Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})
:

We note that the allocation corresponding to the greedy procedure in Step 1 of Algorithm
4
corresponds to a point where both the revenue function and its corresponding MCUA coincide. Consequently, it follows that
\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})=Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})
.

Relation (iv):
Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})\leq Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1})
:

This result directly follows, by definition, from the fact that
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1}=\argmax\{Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}),Q_{R+1}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\}
.

Finally, combining the above four relations that we have shown, we obtain that:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{*})\leq\hat{Q}_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})=Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}^{R+1})\leq Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1}),

which proves our claim that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\leq Q_{R+1}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}_{A}^{R+1})
, i.e., Algorithm
4
with
R+1
resources achieves a total revenue that is at least that corresponding to the optimal solution of Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
). This establishes our claim.

B.10
Proof of Theorem
10

In the following, we prove Theorem
10
using four intermediate lemmas, which we elucidate below. After presenting the statements of these lemmas, we present their proofs in the remainder of this section.

To elucidate our lemmas, we begin by defining the following optimization problem, which rather than maximizing the payoff across all locations, maximizes the sum of the administrator’s payoffs across all sets of locations in the level
\mathcal{L}_{1}
of the hierarchy:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{|\mathcal{L}_{1}|}_{\geq 0}}{\mathrm{max}}\quad\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S}),

(32a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{|\mathcal{L}_{1}|}_{\geq 0}}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}\leq R,

(32b)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{S}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}

(32c)

\displaystyle\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime}}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{t},

(32d)

where, for each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, we define

\displaystyle P_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})=~

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle(\sigma_{l})_{l\in S}}{\mathrm{max}}\quad\sum_{l\in S}P_{l}(\sigma_{l}),

(33a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{(\sigma_{l})_{l\in S}}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},

(33b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\in[0,1].

(33c)

Our first lemma establishes an equivalence between Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
) and the administrator’s payoff maximization Problem (
8a
)-(
8d
) by establishing that the optimal payoff corresponding to both problems is equal.

Lemma 12
.

Suppose that
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
is the optimal solution to Problem (
8a
)-(
8d
) and
(R_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is the optimal solution to Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
). Then, the optimal payoff corresponding to the solutions of both optimization problems are equal, i.e.,
\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})=P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
.

Lemma
12
establishes that, without loss of generality, it suffices to focus our attention on Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
) to establish our desired approximation ratio guarantee. We also note that analogous to Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
), which corresponds to maximizing the administrator’s payoffs, we can formulate an analogous problem of maximizing the MCUA of the payoff function as follows:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{|\mathcal{L}_{1}|}_{\geq 0}}{\mathrm{max}}\quad\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S}),

(34a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\in\mathbbm{R}^{|\mathcal{L}_{1}|}_{\geq 0}}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}\leq R,

(34b)

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{S}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}

(34c)

\displaystyle\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime}}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{t},

(34d)

where, for each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, we define

\displaystyle\hat{P}_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})=~

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle(\sigma_{l})_{l\in S}}{\mathrm{max}}\quad\sum_{l\in S}\hat{P}_{l}(\sigma_{l}),

(35a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{(\sigma_{l})_{l\in S}}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},

(35b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\in[0,1],

(35c)

where we denote
\hat{P}_{l}(\sigma_{l})
as the MCUA of the payoff function
P_{l}(\sigma_{l})
at each location
l
.

Our next lemma establishes that maximizing the MCUA of the payoff function, i.e., solving Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
), results in a higher objective compared to directly maximizing the payoff function, i.e., solving Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
).

Lemma 13
.

Suppose that
(R_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is the optimal solution to Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
) and
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is the optimal solution to Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
). Then, it holds that
\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})\leq\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})
.

Lemma
13
establishes that it suffices to obtain the desired approximation ratio guarantees with respect to the objective of maximizing the MCUA of the payoff functions.

Our third lemma establishes a characterization of the optimal solution of Problem (
35a
)-(
35c
). In particular, Lemma
14
establishes that for any allocation
\tilde{R}_{S}
corresponding to a given set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, a greedy algorithm that allocates resources to locations in set
S
in the descending order of slopes of the MCUA of the payoff functions achieves the optimal solution to Problem (
35a
)-(
35c
).

Lemma 14
.

Suppose
\tilde{R}_{S}
corresponds to the allocation for a set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
and
(\sigma_{l}^{\prime})_{l\in S}
is the allocation computed using step one of Algorithm
7
for the location subset
S
given
\tilde{R}_{S}
resources. Then, it holds that
\hat{P}_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})=\sum_{l\in S}\hat{P}_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{\prime})
.

Lemma
14
is akin to Lemma
9
and thus we omit its proof for brevity.

Our final lemma establishes that Step 1 of the Constrained-Greedy algorithm computes the optimal allocation vector
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
that corresponds to the solution to Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
).

Lemma 15
.

Suppose that
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is the optimal solution to Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
). Then, the resulting allocation computed in step 1 of the Constrained-Greedy algorithm achieves the optimal objective corresponding to the solution of Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
).

We note that Lemma
14
is analogous to several classical results in the literature which establish the optimality of greedy based algorithms when optimizing over a constraint structure that corresponds to a polymatroid (note that a hierarchy is a special case of a polymatroid constraint structure).

Finally, we combine Lemmas
12
-
15
to complete the proof of Theorem
10
in Appendix
B.10.4
.

B.10.1
Proof of Lemma
12

(
\implies
:) Suppose that
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
is an optimal solution to Problem (
8a
)-(
8d
). Then, we show that the optimal objective of Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
) is at least
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
. To see this, let
\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}^{*}
. Then, it clearly holds by the feasibility of
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
that
(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is feasible. To see this, note that:

•

\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}^{*}=\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq R
, where the second equality follows by the definition of
\mathcal{L}_{1}
for which it holds that each location
l
belongs to exactly one set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

•

\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq\lambda_{S},
for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}

•

\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime}}=\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\sum_{l\in S^{\prime}}\sigma_{l}^{*}=\sum_{l\in S}\sigma_{l}^{*}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S}
, for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{t}
, where the second equality follows by the definition of the layer
\mathcal{L}_{1}
for which it holds that each location
l
belongs to exactly one set
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Next, by the definition of
P_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})
in Equations (
33a
)-(
33c
), it follows that
P_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})\geq\sum_{l\in S}P_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{*})
. Thus, we have shown that:

\displaystyle P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\sum_{l\in S}P_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(R_{S}^{*}),

(36)

where (a) follows by the definition of the layers
\mathcal{L}_{1}
for which it holds that each location
l
belongs to exactly one set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, (b) follows from our above established fact that
P_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})\geq\sum_{l\in S}P_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{*})
, and (c) follows by the optimality of
(R_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
for Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
).

The proof of the reverse direction of this claim follows an almost analogous line of reasoning where we establish that the optimal solution of Problem (
32a
)-(
32d
) can be transformed to a feasible solution to Problem (
8a
)-(
8c
). We omit the details of this claim for brevity.

B.10.2
Proof of Lemma
13

Let
(\bar{\sigma}_{l}^{*})_{l\in S}
be the optimal solution corresponding to Problem (
33a
)-(
33c
) given the allocation
R_{S}^{*}
for each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, i.e.,
P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})=\sum_{l\in S}P_{l}(\bar{\sigma}_{l}^{*})
.

Then, to establish this claim, consider the following sequence of inequalities:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\sum_{l\in S}P_{l}(\bar{\sigma}_{l}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\sum_{l\in S}\hat{P}_{l}(\bar{\sigma}_{l}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(R_{S}^{*})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*}),

where (a) follows as
\hat{P}_{l}(\bar{\sigma}_{l}^{*})\geq P_{l}(\bar{\sigma}_{l}^{*})
as
\hat{P}_{l}(\cdot)
is the MCUA of the payoff function
P_{l}(\cdot)
and thus point-wise upper bounds the payoff function, (b) follows by the definition of
\hat{P}_{S}(R_{S}^{*})
, and (c) follows by the optimality of
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
when maximizing the MCUA of the payoff function. This establishes our claim.

B.10.3
Proof of Lemma
15

To prove this claim, consider two solutions
(\hat{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
and
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
, where the first corresponds to the solution of step one of Constrained-Greedy and the latter corresponds to the optimal solution to Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
).

Now, suppose for contradiction that
\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})>\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S})
. This implies that there is some set
S^{\prime}
such that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}\neq\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}
. In particular, there must be some set
S^{\prime}
such that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}>\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}
as the functions
\hat{P}_{S}(\cdot)
are monotonically non-decreasing in their argument for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Furthermore, given the hierarchical nature of the constraint set, where no two constraints in a given layer intersect with each other, we note that the total number of resources allocated under step one of Constrained-Greedy is equal to that under the solution to Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
), i.e.,
\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}
. Consequently, it holds for some set
S^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}<\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}
since for some set
S^{\prime}\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
it holds that that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}<\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}
.

We will now construct another feasible strategy with a strictly higher objective for Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
) than that achieved under the allocation
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
. To do so, we first define an appropriate constant
\epsilon
as follows. To define this constant, we first define
\epsilon_{1}=\min_{|\hat{R}_{S}^{*}-\hat{R}_{S}|>0}|\hat{R}_{S}^{*}-\hat{R}_{S}|
, i.e., the minimum difference between the two solutions for which the solutions have some discrepancy. Furthermore, we define
\epsilon_{\min}=\min_{|\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}|>0}|\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}|
. Note here that for a set
S
, the equality
\sum_{S^{\prime}\subseteq S}\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}=0
can only happen in one of two cases: (i) for all sets
S^{\prime}
, it holds that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}=\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}
or (ii) for some sets
S_{1}
it holds that
\hat{R}_{S_{1}}^{*}>\hat{R}_{S_{1}}
, for some sets
S_{2}
it holds that
\hat{R}_{S_{2}}^{*}=\hat{R}_{S_{2}}
, and for some sets
S_{3}
, it holds that
\hat{R}_{S_{3}}^{*}<\hat{R}_{S_{3}}
. Finally, we define
\epsilon=\min\{\epsilon_{1},\epsilon_{\min}\}
. Note by definition that
\epsilon>0
.

Having defined
\epsilon
, to construct a feasible strategy with a strictly higher objective for Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
) than that achieved under the allocation
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
, we now select two sets
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}
. To define these two sets, first consider all sets
S
in the layer
\mathcal{L}_{2}
and check if there is any set that contains both a set
S^{\prime}
such that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}>\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}
and a set
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}<\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}
. If there is not such set in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, we proceed to layer
\mathcal{L}_{3}
and so on. Note this process certainly terminates at layer
\mathcal{L}_{t+1}
which corresponds to the overall resource constraint by our assumption that there exists some
S^{\prime}
such that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}>\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}
and a set
S^{\prime\prime}
such that
\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}<\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}
. Thus, define
\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}}
as the first layer at which the above-mentioned phenomena occurs.

Then, define allocation
(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
such that
\tilde{R}_{S}=\hat{R}_{S}^{*}
for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\backslash(S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime})
,
\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime}}=\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon
and
\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}=\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}+\epsilon
.
We first show that this new strategy is feasible:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\backslash(S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime})}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}+\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon+\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}+\epsilon=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}\leq R,

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{S}=\hat{R}_{S}^{*}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S},\quad\forall S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\backslash(S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime}),

\displaystyle\bar{\lambda}_{S^{\prime}}\geq\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}\geq\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon=\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime}}\implies\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime}}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S^{\prime}},

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}=\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}+\epsilon\leq\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S^{\prime\prime}}\implies\tilde{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S^{\prime\prime}},

Moreover for all layers
i\geq i^{\prime}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S_{1}},\forall S_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{t},\text{ s.t. }S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\notin S_{1}

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in S_{1}\backslash(S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime})}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}+\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon+\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}+\epsilon=\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S_{1}},\forall S_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{t},\text{ s.t. }S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\in S_{1},

as either
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\in S_{1}
or
S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\notin S_{1}
for all layers
i\geq i^{\prime}
.

Next, for
i<i^{\prime}
, it holds that:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S_{1}},\forall S_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}},\text{ s.t. }S^{\prime},S^{\prime\prime}\notin S_{1}

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in S_{1}\backslash S^{\prime}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}+\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon\leq\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S_{1}},\forall S_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}},\text{ s.t. }S^{\prime}\in S_{1},S^{\prime\prime}\notin S_{1},

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\tilde{R}_{S}=\sum_{S\in S_{1}\backslash S^{\prime\prime}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}+\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}+\epsilon=\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}+\epsilon\leq\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}\leq\bar{\lambda}_{S_{1}},\forall S_{1}\subseteq\mathcal{L}_{2}\cup\ldots\cup\mathcal{L}_{i^{\prime}},\text{ s.t. }S^{\prime}\notin S_{1},S^{\prime\prime}\in S_{1},

where the inequality that
\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}^{*}+\epsilon\leq\sum_{S\in S_{1}}\hat{R}_{S}
follows from our construction of
i^{\prime}
and also the definition of
\epsilon
. Thus, we have established that the allocation
(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is feasible.

Finally, we show that the objective of Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
) is greater under the allocation
(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
. To see this, first recall by Lemma
14
that the objective
\hat{P}_{S}(R_{S})=\sum_{l\in S}\hat{P}_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})
for any set
S
and allocation
R_{S}
, where
(\hat{\sigma}_{l})_{l\in S}
is given by the solution of a greedy procedure corresponding to the step one of Algorithm
7
for the location subset
S
given
R_{S}
resources. Furthermore, let
c_{S^{\prime}}
be the maximum slope of the segments corresponding to set
S^{\prime}
that have been allocated resources under the optimal allocation but not allocated resources under step one of Constrained-Greedy and let
c_{S^{\prime\prime}}
be the minimum slope of the segments corresponding to the set
S^{\prime\prime}
that have been allocated resources under step one of Constrained-Greedy but not allocated resources under the optimal allocation. For simplicity of exposition, we assume that the slopes of all segments of the MCUA of the payoff function are distinct (and, in particular, that
c_{S^{\prime\prime}}>c_{S^{\prime}}
) but note that the proof can readily be generalized to the setting when this condition is not necessarily met. Then, we have that:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\tilde{R}_{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\backslash(S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime})}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})+\hat{P}_{S^{\prime}}(\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*}-\epsilon)+\hat{P}_{S^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*}+\epsilon),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}\backslash(S^{\prime}\cup S^{\prime\prime})}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})+\hat{P}_{S^{\prime}}(\hat{R}_{S^{\prime}}^{*})-\epsilon c_{S^{\prime}}+\hat{P}_{S^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{R}_{S^{\prime\prime}}^{*})+\epsilon c_{S^{\prime\prime}},

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})+\epsilon(c_{S^{\prime\prime}}-c_{S^{\prime}}),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{>}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*}),

where (a) follows from the definition of
c_{S^{\prime}}
and
c_{S^{\prime\prime}}
and (b) follows as
c_{S^{\prime\prime}}>c_{S^{\prime}}
due to the nature of step one of Constrained-Greedy which allocates resources in the descending order of the slopes of the segments. Thus, we have obtained that the objective under the new allocation
(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
is greater than that under
(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
, a contradiction. Hence, we have shown that the step one of Constrained-Greedy indeed computes the optimal allocations corresponding to the solution to Problem (
34a
)-(
34d
), which establishes our claim.

B.10.4
Completing Proof of Theorem
10

We establish the approximation ratio guarantee in the statement of Theorem
10
for the allocation
\bm{\sigma}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|}^{A}
and note that the approximation ratio guarantee for the allocation
\bm{\sigma}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|}^{A}
follows similarly.

To establish the approximation ratio guarantee for the allocation
\bm{\sigma}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|}^{A}
, we begin by noting the structure of the solution of Constrained-Greedy. In particular, the solution of the first stage of Constrained-Greedy is such that for all sets
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, one of the following three conditions holds:

1.

The total allocation to set
S
is equal to the upper bound
\bar{\lambda}_{S}

2.

The condition in case (i) does not hold and for each location
l\in S
, either
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=0
or
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for some
i\in\mathcal{I}

3.

The condition in case (i) does not hold and for at most one location
l\in S
it holds that
\hat{\sigma}_{l}\neq 0
and
\hat{\sigma}_{l}\neq\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
. For all other locations in the set
S
, it holds that either
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=0
or
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for some
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

Note that there can be at most
|\mathcal{L}_{2}|
sets
S
belonging to case three. We now consider each of the above three cases in turn.

Case (i):

Suppose that under stage one of the Constrained-Greedy algorithm the total allocation to a set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
is equal to the upper bound
\bar{\lambda}_{S}
. In this case, it follows from Lemma
14
that
\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})=\sum_{l\in S}\hat{P}_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})=\sum_{l\in S_{1}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+\hat{P}_{l^{\prime}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}})
, where
S=S_{1}\cup\{l^{\prime}\}
and the second equality follows from the fact that the allocation in stage one will coincide with the original payoff function for all but at most one location
l^{\prime}
in each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Next, without relaxing any of the upper bound quotas corresponding to the constraint sets in case (i), we note that the allocation computed in stage two of Constrained-Greedy achieves at least half the objective
\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})
, i.e.,
P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})\geq\frac{1}{2}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})
, which follows similar arguments to our earlier obtained half approximation guarantees (e.g., see proof of Theorem
7
).

Case (ii):

Suppose that under step 1 of the Constrained-Greedy algorithm the total allocation to a set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
is strictly less than the upper bound
\bar{\lambda}_{S}
and that case (ii) does not hold. Moreover, suppose for each location
l\in S
that either
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=0
or
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for some
i
. In this case, it holds for some location set
S_{1}\subseteq S
that
\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})=\sum_{l\in S_{1}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})
. Note that with
R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|
resources, it holds that the greedy algorithm allocates at least
\hat{R}_{S}^{*}
to the locations in the set
S
. Consequently, it holds that
P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|,l})_{l\in S})\geq P_{S}((\tilde{\sigma}^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|,l})_{l\in S})\geq P_{S}((\tilde{\sigma}^{A}_{R,l})_{l\in S})=\sum_{l\in S_{1}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})=\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})
, where
(\tilde{\sigma}^{A}_{R,l})_{l\in S}
(
(\tilde{\sigma}^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S}
) represents the allocation computed in step one of Algorithm
7
in stage two of Constrained-Greedy for the set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
given
R
resources (
R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|
resources and an associated relaxation of the upper bound quotas in the constraint hierarchy).

Case (iii):

Finally, suppose that under step 1 of the Constrained-Greedy algorithm neither of cases (i) or (ii) hold and for at most one location
l\in S
it holds that
\hat{\sigma}_{l}\neq 0
and
\hat{\sigma}_{l}\neq\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for all
i\in\mathcal{I}
. For all other locations in the set
S
, it holds that either
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=0
or
\hat{\sigma}_{l}=\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
for some
i\in\mathcal{I}
. In this case, it holds that
\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})=\sum_{l\in H_{3}^{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+\hat{P}_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}})
, where
S=H_{3}^{S}\cup\{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}\}
for some set
H_{3}^{S}
and location
l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}
specific to the set
S
.

Thus, denoting
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{j}
as the set of locations corresponding to case
j
above for
j\in\{1,2,3\}
, it follows that:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})

\displaystyle=\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{2}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*}),

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\leq}}\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{1}}2P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{2}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}}\left(\sum_{l\in H_{3}^{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+\hat{P}_{l^{\prime\prime}_{S}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime\prime}_{S}})\right),

where (a) follows from our analysis of the three cases above. Next, noting that if we relax the upper bound constraints in the set
l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}
for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}
by one unit, where we recall that
|\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}|\leq|\mathcal{L}_{2}|
, following arguments similar to our earlier established resource augmentation guarantees (e.g., see proof of Theorem
8
), the resulting allocation computed in stage two of Constrained-Greedy given
R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|
resources with the relaxation of the upper bound quotas will yield the following inequality:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{1}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{2}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}}\left(\sum_{l\in H_{3}^{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+P_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}})\right),

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{1}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{2}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})+\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S}),

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S})

where the first inequality follows as
\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}}\geq\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime\prime}_{S}}
due to the relaxation of the upper bound quotas and the second inequality follows as the allocation computed via Constrained-Greedy for the sets in
\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}
achieves at least the objective as
P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+P_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}}(\tilde{\sigma}_{l^{{}^{\prime\prime}}_{S}})
for each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}^{3}
.

Finally, applying Lemma
13
establishes our desired half approximation guarantee as:

\displaystyle\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})\leq\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\leq 2\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}((\sigma^{A}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{2}|,l})_{l\in S}),

which establishes our claim. We note that we can apply a similar argument to the one above to establish the desired one-approximation guarantee for the allocation
\bm{\sigma}_{R+|\mathcal{L}_{1}|}^{A}
and we omit this for brevity.

B.11
Extending Constrained-Greedy to Lower Bound Constraints

We generalize of Constrained-Greedy presented in Algorithm
5
to the setting with lower bound constraints. In presenting this generalization, we assume that a feasible allocation that satisfies the constraints of Problem (
8a
)-(
8d
) exists. Then, in stage one of Constrained-Greedy, after computing the MCUA of the payoff functions and ordering the segments in the descending order of their slopes, we first ensure that we satisfy all the lower bound constraints as follows:

•

For all sets in
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, allocate resources greedily in the descending order of the slopes of the MCUA of the payoff functions to the respective sets of locations to exactly satisfy the lower bound constraint for each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

•

Given the allocations in the first step to satisfy the lower bound for the layer
\mathcal{L}_{1}
, then allocate resources to satisfy lower bound constraint in layer
\mathcal{L}_{2}
without violating any upper bound constraints in the process of doing so.

•

Continue this process in the increasing depth of the layers from
\mathcal{L}_{3}
to
\mathcal{L}_{t}
until all lower bound constrains are satisfied.

Finally, once all lower bound constraints across all layers are satisfied, we proceed by applying stage one of Constrained-Greedy as presented in Algorithm
5
, given the already allocated resources to satisfy the lower bound constraints. We output the corresponding allocation
\hat{\bm{\sigma}}
as the final allocation.

Thus, incorporating lower bound constraints involves two modifications to Algorithm
5
, which applies to upper bound constraints. First, we require an initial processing step to ensure the allocation computed in the first stage of Constrained-Greedy meets the lower bound constraints across layers. Next, the allocation computed using stage one is the final allocation, unlike the Constrained-Greedy algorithm with upper bound constraints (that has a second stage), as applying the sub-routine corresponding to Algorithm
7
is only guaranteed to satisfy an upper bound resource constraint and thus may violate the lower bound constraints.

B.12
Key Ideas in Proof of Proposition
2

The proof of Proposition
2
follows similar arguments to that in the proof of Theorem
10
. In the following, we note some caveats required to prove Proposition
2
.

To this end, first note that the corresponding analogues of Lemmas
12
,
13
, and
14
naturally hold in the setting with lower bound constraints. Moreover, the corresponding analogue of Lemma
15
also holds in the setting with lower bound constraints, with the additional caveat that we also need to show that the new solution
(\tilde{R}_{S})_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}
we construct also satisfies the lower bound constraints, which follows similar arguments to the satisfaction of the upper bound constraints in the proof of Lemma
15
. We note that establishing the analogues of Lemmas
12
-
15
applies for any class of lower bound constraints and does not rely on the assumption that the lower bound quotas for all sets
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
are two.

Finally, we leverage the fact that the lower bound quotas are such that
\underline{\lambda}_{S}\geq 2
for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
. To this end, first note that the optimal objective for each location set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
satisfies:
\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})=\sum_{l\in S}\hat{P}_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})=\sum_{l\in H_{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+\hat{P}_{l^{\prime}_{S}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}_{S}})
, where
S=H_{S}\cup\{l^{\prime}_{S}\}
and the second equality follows from the fact that the allocation in stage one will coincide with the original payoff function for all but at most one location
l^{\prime}_{S}
in each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
.

Next, fix any set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Note that the solution corresponding to maximizing the MCUA of the payoff function achieves an outcome with a payoff given by
P_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\geq\sum_{l\in H_{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})
.

Next, let
P_{S}^{*,1}
correspond to the maximum payoff corresponding to spending all resources to a single location in a set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
and let
P_{S}^{*,R=1}
represent optimal payoff corresponding to spending one unit of resources to a set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, where note that
P_{S}^{*,R=1}\geq P_{S}^{*,1}\geq\hat{P}_{l^{\prime}_{S}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}_{S}})
. Finally, since we have at least two resources being allocated to each
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
since
\underline{\lambda}_{S}=2
for all
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, it follows that
\sum_{l\in H_{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})\geq P_{S}^{*,R=1}
by our earlier analysis for our resource augmentation results (e.g., see proof of Theorem
7
).

Thus, from the above two derived relations we have that
P_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\geq\sum_{l\in H_{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})
and that
P_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\geq P_{S}^{*,R=1}\geq\hat{P}_{l^{\prime}_{S}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}_{S}})
, which implies that
2P_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\geq\sum_{l\in H_{S}}P_{l}(\hat{\sigma}_{l})+\hat{P}_{l^{\prime}_{S}}(\hat{\sigma}_{l^{\prime}_{S}})=\hat{P}_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\geq P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})
for each set
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
. Consequently, summing the above relation for all sets
S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}
, we obtain our desired approximation ratio guarantee that
2\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(\hat{R}_{S}^{*})\geq\sum_{S\in\mathcal{L}_{1}}P_{S}(R_{S}^{*})
, which establishes our claim.

Appendix C
Payoff Maximization with Heterogeneous Users

In this section, we extend our results obtained in the setting with heterogeneous users under the revenue maximization objective to the payoff maximization objective. To this end, in the following, we present the associated greedy algorithm for the administrator’s payoff maximization objective and its corresponding approximation ratio and resource augmentation guarantees.

In studying the payoff maximization problem of the administrator with heterogeneous user types, we first note that the problem of computing the administrator’s payoff-maximizing strategy in the setting with heterogeneous users is NP-hard, which follows by our hardness result on solving for the administrator’s payoff-maximizing strategy in the setting with homogeneous users (Theorem
2
). Thus, in this section, we introduce a greedy algorithm, described in Algorithm
7
, to approximately solve for the administrator’s payoff maximizing resource allocation strategy and highlight its corresponding approximation ratio and resource augmentation guarantees.

We begin by first describing our algorithmic approach to achieve an approximate solution to the administrator’s payoff maximization problem, which proceeds as follows. First, rather than directly optimizing the payoff of the administrator, which as noted earlier is NP-hard to optimize, we maximize its corresponding monotone concave upper approximation (MCUA), which we depict in Figure
8
. In particular, similar of maximizing the MCUA of the revenue function in the presence of heterogeneous user types (see Section
5.2
), optimizing the MCUA of the payoff function can be reduced to solving a linear program whose solution boils down to a greedy-like procedure presented in Step 1 of Algorithm
7
(see Theorem
11
).

To elucidate this greedy-like procedure, we first define an affordability threshold
t_{l}=\min\left\{R,\max_{i}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}\right\}
for each location
l
. Next, we define the MCUA of the payoff function over the range
[0,t_{l}]
for each location
l
, which we note is a piece-wise linear function; thus, we define
\mathcal{S}
as the set of all such piece-wise linear segments of the MCUA of the payoff function across all locations. We then characterize each segment
s\in\mathcal{S}
by three parameters: (i)
l_{s}
, which represents the location corresponding to segment
s
, (ii)
c_{s}
, which corresponds to the slope of segment
s
, and (iii)
x_{s}
, which represents the horizontal width, i.e., resource requirement, of segment
s
. In particular, Figure
8
(right) depicts the associated slopes
c_{s}
and resource requirements
x_{s}
corresponding to the segments for the MCUA of the payoff function for a given location
l
. We then order the segments in the set
\mathcal{S}
in the descending order of the slopes of the MCUA of the payoff function and find the solution corresponding to a greedy algorithm that allocates at most
z_{s}
to each segment in the descending order of the slopes of the MCUA of the payoff function.

Next, as with Algorithm
4
, we compute an allocation corresponding to spending all the available resources at a single location that yields the highest payoff to the administrator. Finally, we return the best of the greedy allocation corresponding to optimizing the MCUA of the payoff function and the allocation corresponding to spending all the available resources at a single location that achieves a higher payoff for the administrator. This procedure is formally presented in Algorithm
7
.

Input :
Total Resource capacity
R
, User Types
\Theta_{l}^{i}=(\Lambda_{l}^{i},d_{l}^{i},p_{l}^{i})
for all locations
l
and types
i

Output :
Resource Allocation Strategy
\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*}

Step 1: Greedy Allocation Based on Slopes of MCUA of Payoff Function:

Define affordability threshold
t_{l}\leftarrow\min\{R,\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\}
for all locations
l
;

Generate MCUA of the payoff function in range
[0,t_{l}]
for each location
l
;

\tilde{\mathcal{S}}\leftarrow
Ordered list of segments
s
across all locations of this MCUA in descending order of slopes
c_{s}
;

Initialize allocation strategy
\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}\leftarrow\mathbf{0}
;

for

segment
s\in\tilde{\mathcal{S}}

do

\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s}}\leftarrow\tilde{\sigma}_{l_{s}}+\min\{R,x_{s}\}
;
Allocate
x_{s}
to location
l_{s}
;

R\leftarrow R-\min\{R,x_{s}\}
;
Update amount of remaining resources
;

end for

Step 2: Find Solution
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
that maximizes payoff from spending on single location:

\bm{\sigma}^{l}\leftarrow\argmax_{\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}:\sigma_{l^{\prime}}=0,\forall l^{\prime}\neq l}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma})
for all locations
l
;

\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}\leftarrow\argmax_{l\in L}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{l})
;

Step 3: Return Solution with a Higher Payoff:

\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A}\leftarrow\argmax\{P_{R}(\tilde{\bm{\sigma}}),P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime})\}
;

Algorithm 7

Greedy Algorithm for Administrator’s Heterogeneous Payoff Maximization Objective

Figure 8
:
Depiction of the payoff as a function of the amount of resources allocated to location
l
(left), its upper bound (center), and its corresponding monotone concave upper approximation (right) for a setting with five types, i.e.,
|\mathcal{I}|=5
. Note that the payoff remains constant after a resource allocation that exceeds
\max_{i}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
. Further, for the MCUA of the payoff function, there are three segments
s
for location
l
, with corresponding slopes
c_{s}
of the segments and the corresponding width
x_{s}
of the segments marked in the plot on the right.

We now present the main results of this section, which establish the approximation guarantees of Algorithm
7
to the optimal payoff of the administrator. Our first result establishes that Algorithm
7
achieves at least half the payoff as that corresponding to the solution of Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
).

Theorem 11

(1/2 Approximation of Greedy Algorithm for Heterogeneous Payoff Maximization)
.

Denote
\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A}
as the solution corresponding to Algorithm
7
and let
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
be the payoff maximizing allocation that solves Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
). Then,
\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A}
achieves at least half the payoff as compared to
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
, i.e.,
P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}_{A}^{*})\geq\frac{1}{2}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
.

Our next result establishes that if the administrator had just one additional resource, i.e.,
R+1
resources, then Algorithm
7
will achieve at least the payoff as the payoff-maximizing solution of the NP-hard bi-level Program (
3a
)-(
3b
) with
R
resources.

Theorem 12

(Resource Augmentation Guarantee for Payoff Maximization)
.

Denote
\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A}
as the solution corresponding to Algorithm
7
with
R+1
resources and let
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
be the payoff-maximizing allocation that solves Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) with
R
resources. Then, the payoff under the allocation
\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A}
with
R+1
resources is at least that corresponding to
\bm{\sigma}^{*}
with
R
resources, i.e.,
P_{R+1}(\bm{\sigma}^{*}_{A})\geq P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{*})
.

We note that the administrator only requires
R+\max_{l\in L,i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}
resources to obtain the resource augmentation guarantee in Theorem
3
; however, we present the result with
R+1
resources for ease of exposition. Theorems
11
and
12
imply that Algorithm
7
obtains the same approximation ratio and resource augmentation guarantees for the administrator’s payoff maximization problem in the setting with heterogeneous users as Algorithm
2
achieved in the setting with homogeneous users (see Section
4.2.3
). Furthermore, we note that Algorithm
7
in the payoff maximization setting follows a similar idea to Algorithm
4
in the revenue maximization setting in terms of generating a monotone concave upper approximation to reduce the originally NP-hard bi-level optimization into a tractable linear program that can be solved with a greedy like procedure. Consequently, the proofs of Theorems
11
and
12
follow similarly to the corresponding results in the revenue maximization setting with heterogeneous user types; hence, we omit these proofs for brevity.

Yet, we note that while Algorithm
7
in the payoff maximization setting is akin to Algorithm
4
in the revenue maximization setting, there is one key point of difference. In particular, unlike in Algorithm
4
, we do not terminate the greedy procedure at the point in the algorithm when there is a segment in the ordered set
\tilde{\mathcal{S}}
such that the segment’s resource requirement
x_{s}
exceeds the available resources. Such a termination of the greedy procedure in step one of Algorithm
7
is not necessary in the payoff maximization setting, as, unlike the revenue function, the payoff function is monotonically (non)-decreasing in the amount of resources allocated to each location (see Figures
2
and
8
). Yet, we note from our analysis in the revenue maximization setting that the obtained approximation ratio and resource augmentation guarantees obtained in Theorems
11
and
12
would continue to hold even in the setting when the greedy procedure in step 1 of Algorithm
7
is terminated in a similar manner to Algorithm
4
.

Finally, we note that Theorems
11
and
12
further highlight the benefit to administrators for recruiting one additional security personnel and applying a simple algorithm, i.e., Algorithm
7
, rather than investing significant computational power and effort to compute the NP-hard payoff-maximizing strategy of the administrator.

Appendix D
Model Extensions

In this section, we present several natural extensions to the model studied in this work.

D.1
Extension to Continuous Set of User Types

In this section, we present the additional notation and the regularity condition necessary to extend the results obtained in this work for a discrete set of user types to a continuous set of user types. For brevity, we present the regularity condition for the payoff maximization setting and note that an analogous condition can be developed for the revenue maximization setting as well.

To define our security game in the setting with a continuous set of user types at each location, we define the total mass of users at a location
l
as
\Lambda_{l}
where each user belongs to a
type

\Theta_{l}^{i}=(d_{l}^{i},p_{l}^{i})
, where
d_{l}^{i}
corresponds to the benefit received by users of type
i
who engage in fraud, and
p_{l}^{i}
represents the payoff to the administrator for allocating a security resource to mitigate fraud at location
l
for users of type
i
. The two dimensions of the users’ type vectors at each location
l
have joint distribution in the population that is known to the administrator.

Let
P_{l}(\cdot)
be a (potentially) non-convex and discontinuous payoff function at a given location
l
and let
t_{l}=\min\left\{R,\max_{i}\frac{d_{l}^{i}}{d_{l}^{i}+k}\right\}
be the maximum amount of resources the administrator can feasibly allocate to location
l
. Then, we make the following regularity assumption on the boundedness of the payoff function.

Assumption 1

(Boundedness of Payoff Function)
.

There exists an affine function that upper bounds payoff function, i.e., there exists some bounded
r
such that
P_{l}(0)+\sigma_{l}r\geq P_{l}(\sigma_{l})
for all
\sigma_{l}\in[0,t_{l}]
.

Assumption
1
imposes a mild regularity condition on the administrator’s payoff function and states that the administrator’s payoff function can be upper bounded by an affine function with some bounded slope
r
. Note that Assumption
1
, in particular, holds for discrete distributions with finite support. We note that Assumption
1
is crucial in ensuring the existence of the super-gradients of the MCUA of the payoff function at all points in the domain
[0,t_{l}]
and in ensuring that the MCUA of the payoff function coincides with the original payoff function at its endpoints even for continuous distributions, as is elucidated through the following lemma.

Lemma 16

(MCUA Coincides with Payoff Function at Endpoints)
.

Let
\hat{P}_{l}(\sigma_{l})
be the MCUA of the payoff function
P_{l}(\sigma_{l})
and that Assumption
1
holds. Then, the MCUA of the payoff function coincides with the payoff function at its endpoints, i.e.,
\hat{P}_{l}(0)=P_{l}(0)
and
\hat{P}_{l}(t_{l})=P_{l}(t_{l})
.

We note that Lemma
16
in the heterogeneous payoff maximization setting with continuously distributed user types is akin to Lemma
11
in the heterogeneous revenue maximization setting with discrete user types. Moreover, since the proof of Lemma
16
is almost entirely analogous to that of Lemma
11
, we omit it for brevity.

Given the regularity condition in Assumption
1
, which guarantees that the MCUA of the payoff function coincides with the payoff function at its endpoints (Lemma
16
), we note that our results and analysis in the setting with discrete user types at each location can be naturally extended to the continuous user type setting with one caveat. In particular, rather than the MCUA of the payoff function corresponding to finitely many segments, as in the setting with discrete user types at each location, the MCUA of the payoff function in the continuous user type setting corresponds to an arbitrary monotonically increasing concave function, which has affine segments at the points at which the MCUA does not coincide with the payoff function. Then, our earlier developed algorithms that achieve the half approximation and resource augmentation guarantees, e.g., Algorithms
4
and
7
, can be modified such that resources are allocated to locations in the descending order of the minimum super-gradients of the MCUA of the payoff functions, which generalizes allocating resources to locations in the descending order of the slopes of the MCUA of the payoff functions in the setting with discrete types to the continuous user type setting. With the above modification to our earlier developed greedy-based algorithms, under Assumption
1
, our obtained half approximation and resource augmentation guarantees hold even in the setting with a continuous set of user types with almost entirely analogous proofs to that in the discrete user type setting. For brevity, we omit presenting the extensions of the results obtained in the discrete user types setting to the continuous user types setting.

D.2
Extension to Probabilistic Setting

This section presents the additional notation required to extend our security game to the Bayesian (or probabilistic) setting, where user types are not deterministic but can be drawn from some distribution.

To model the probabilistic setting, we let
\mathcal{I}
denote the set of user
groups
and assume that a user type
\Theta_{l}^{i}
at each location
l
is drawn independently from some discrete probability distribution with finite support
(\Theta_{l}^{i,j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}=(\Lambda_{l}^{i,j},d_{l}^{i,j},p_{l}^{i,j})_{j\in\mathcal{J}}
, where the support, defined by
\mathcal{J}
, satisfies
|\mathcal{J}|\in\mathbb{N}
. We define the probability that a user group
i
at location
l
has a type
\Theta_{l}^{i,j}
as
q_{l}^{i,j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
. In other words,
\mathbb{P}(\Theta_{l}^{i}=\Theta_{l}^{i,j})=q_{l}^{i,j}
for all
j\in\mathcal{J}
, where
q_{l}^{i,j}\geq 0
and
\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}q_{l}^{i,j}=1
for all locations
l\in L
and user groups
i\in\mathcal{I}
.

In this setting, in line with Bayesian Stackelberg games
[
24
]
, we assume that while users at each location know the realization of the type
j\in\mathcal{J}
at that location, the administrator only knows the distribution of each user group’s type. Consequently, denoting the best response of users in the group
i
of type
j
at location
l
as
y_{l}^{i,j}(\bm{\sigma})
, given an administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}
, we formulate the following expected revenue maximization problem (ERMP) of the administrator:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}\\
y_{l}^{i}(\bm{\sigma})\in[0,1],\forall l\in L,i\in\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}{\mathrm{max}}\quad Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma})=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}q_{l}^{i,j}\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}y_{l}^{i,j}(\bm{\sigma})k\Lambda_{l}^{i,j},

(37a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{\sigma}\in\Omega_{R}\\
y_{l}^{i}(\bm{\sigma})\in[0,1],\forall l\in L,i\in\mathcal{I}\end{subarray}}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle y_{l}^{i,j}(\bm{\sigma})\in\argmax_{y\in[0,1]}U_{l}^{i,j}(\bm{\sigma},y)=y[(1-\sigma_{l})d_{l}^{i,j}-\sigma_{l}k],\quad\text{for all }l\in L,i\in\mathcal{I},j\in\mathcal{J}

(37b)

where in upper-level problem, the administrator uses a strategy
\bm{\sigma}
that maximizes its expected revenue to which the users of each group at each location best respond by maximizing their utilities in the lower-level problem based on the realized type. We note that an analogous formulation for the administrator’s expected payoff maximization problem can also be derived.

Note that the above ERMP exactly corresponds to the revenue maximization Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
) in the deterministic setting if we consider a type space
|\mathcal{I}|\times|\mathcal{J}|
, where the number of users belonging to a type
(i,j)
at any location
l
is given by
q_{l}^{i,j}\Lambda_{l}^{i,j}
. Analogously, the administrator’s expected payoff maximization problem exactly corresponds to the payoff maximization Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) in the deterministic setting if we consider a type space
|\mathcal{I}|\times|\mathcal{J}|
, where the administrator payoff corresponding to preventing fraud from users belonging to a type
(i,j)
at any location
l
is given by
q_{l}^{i,j}p_{l}^{i,j}
. Given this transformation from the probabilistic setting to the deterministic setting, any algorithms and corresponding guarantees derived in this work for the deterministic setting extend to solving the above-defined ERMP and the administrator’s expected payoff maximization problem.

D.3
Extension to Setting where Administrator can Optimize over Fines

In this section, we consider the setting when the fines in the system are not fixed, and the administrator is tasked with the problem of not only computing a resource allocation strategy but also the fine from an interval
k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]
, for some constants
\underline{k},\bar{k}>0
. In both the payoff and revenue maximization settings, we show that the optimal strategy for the administrator is to always set the fine to the maximum allowable level, i.e.,
k=\bar{k}
. For brevity of notation and expositional simplicity, we prove these results in the setting with homogeneous user types and thus drop
i
in the superscript of the notation of user types.

D.3.1
Variability of Fines under Payoff Maximization Objective

We first consider the administrator’s payoff maximization objective. In particular, defining
P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k)
as the payoff function when
\sigma_{l}
resources are allocated to location
l
under a fine
k
, the administrator’s payoff maximization Problem (
3a
)-(
3b
) can be formulated as the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\bm{\sigma},k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]}{\mathrm{max}}\quad P_{R}(\bm{\sigma},k)=\sum_{l\in L}P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k),

(38a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\bm{\sigma},k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}\leq R,

(38b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\in[0,1],\quad\forall l\in L

(38c)

We now show that setting the fine
k=\bar{k}
is an optimal solution to the above problem.

To prove this claim, fix fines
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
and let
\bm{\sigma}^{k_{1}}
and
\bm{\sigma}^{k_{2}}
be the corresponding optimal resource allocation strategies that maximize the administrator’s payoff under the fines
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, respectively. Furthermore, without loss of generality, let
k_{1}<k_{2}
. Then, it is straightforward to see that the inequality
P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{1})\leq P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{2})
holds for the payoff functions under the two fines for all
\sigma_{l}\in[0,1]
. In particular, since
k_{1}<k_{2}
, we note that
P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{1})=P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{2})
for
\sigma_{l}<\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{2}}
and for
\sigma_{l}\geq\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{1}}
, but
P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{1})<P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{2})
for
\sigma_{l}\in[\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{2}},\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{1}})
. Consequently, it holds that
P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{1})\leq P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{2})
for all
\sigma_{l}\in[0,1]
.

Then, it follows that:

\displaystyle P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{k_{2}},k_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}_{k_{1}},k_{2})=\sum_{l\in L}P_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{k_{1}},k_{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\geq}}\sum_{l\in L}P_{l}(\sigma_{l}^{k_{1}},k_{1})=P_{R}(\bm{\sigma}_{k_{1}},k_{1}),

where (a) follows by the optimality of
\bm{\sigma}^{k_{2}}
under the fine
k_{2}
and (b) follows from our above analysis that
P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{1})\leq P_{l}(\sigma_{l},k_{2})
for all
\sigma_{l}\in[0,1]
. Consequently, our analysis establishes that the payoff is monotonically non-decreasing in the fine, which implies that setting the fine to
k=\bar{k}
is optimal in terms of maximizing the administrator’s payoff.

We also note that the above analysis even extends to the setting when fines are allowed to vary across locations.

D.3.2
Variability of Fines Under Revenue Maximization Objective

This section considers the administrator’s revenue maximization objective. In this setting, we define
Q_{l}(\sigma_{l},k)
as the revenue function when
\sigma_{l}
resources are allocated to location
l
under a fine
k
. Then, the administrator’s revenue maximization Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
) can be formulated as the following optimization problem:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\bm{\sigma},k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]}{\mathrm{max}}\quad Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma},k)=\sum_{l\in L}Q_{l}(\sigma_{l},k),

(39a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\bm{\sigma},k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}\leq R,

(39b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\in[0,1],\quad\forall l\in L

(39c)

Note from Lemma
1
that the above problem can be reformulated as:

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\bm{\sigma},k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]}{\mathrm{max}}\quad Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma},k)=\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}k\Lambda_{l},

(40a)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\bm{\sigma},k\in[\underline{k},\bar{k}]}}{\mathrm{max}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{max}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{l\in L}\sigma_{l}\leq R,

(40b)

\displaystyle\sigma_{l}\in\left[0,\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\right],\quad\forall l\in L

(40c)

We now show that setting the fine
k=\bar{k}
is an optimal solution to the above problem.

To prove this claim, first note by the optimality of Algorithm
1
that the optimal administrator revenue for a fixed fine
k
is given by:

\displaystyle Q_{R}^{*}(k)=\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k}k\Lambda_{1}+\ldots+\frac{d_{l_{k}}}{d_{l_{k}}+k}k\Lambda_{l_{k}}+\left(R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\right)k\Lambda_{l_{k}+1},

where the locations are ordered in descending order of
\Lambda_{l}
and
\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}<R
but
\sum_{l\in[l_{k}+1]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}>R
for some
l_{k}
. Note also that any change in the fines does not influence the ordering of the locations under the optimal allocation.

Now, we compute the derivative of the above objective with the fine to get:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial Q_{R}^{*}(k)}{\partial k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\left(\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\Lambda_{l}-\frac{k\Lambda_{l}d_{l}}{(d_{l}+k)^{2}}\right)+\Lambda_{l_{k}+1}\left(R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\right)+\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\frac{k\Lambda_{l_{k}+1}d_{l}}{(d_{l}+k)^{2}},

\displaystyle=R\Lambda_{l_{k}+1}+\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\left(\frac{d_{l}^{2}\Lambda_{l}}{(d_{l}+k)^{2}}-\frac{d_{l}^{2}\Lambda_{L_{k}+1}}{(d_{l}+k)^{2}}\right),

\displaystyle=R\Lambda_{l_{k}+1}+\sum_{l\in[l_{k}]}\frac{d_{l}^{2}}{(d_{l}+k)^{2}}(\Lambda_{l}-\Lambda_{l_{k}+1}),

\displaystyle\geq 0,

where the final inequality follows as
\Lambda_{l}\geq\Lambda_{l_{k}+1}
for all
l\in[l_{k}]
, as we ordered locations in the descending order of the
\Lambda_{l}
values in Algorithm
1
.

The above analysis implies that the revenue is monotonically non-decreasing in the fine, which implies that setting the fine to
k=\bar{k}
is optimal in terms of maximizing the administrator’s revenues. We note that a similar analysis can be performed even for the heterogeneous revenue maximization setting by applying the structure of the optimal solution of the administrator’s revenue maximization Problem (
2a
)-(
2b
) established in Lemma
7
.

D.3.3
Variability of Fines Under Revenue Maximization Objective where Fines can vary across Locations

This section extends the result obtained in the previous section for the administrator’s revenue maximization problem to the setting when the fines can vary across locations. In particular, consider fines
(k_{l})_{l\in L}
, where for one location
l^{\prime}
, we let the fines vary such that
k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}<k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}
and let the fines of all other locations be fixed. Then, the optimal administrator revenue under the fine vector
\mathbf{k}^{1}=(k_{l}^{1})_{l\in L}
is given by:

\displaystyle Q_{R}^{*}(\textbf{k}^{1})=\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k_{1}^{1}}k_{1}^{1}\Lambda_{1}+\ldots+\frac{d_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}}{d_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}+k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}}k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}+\left(R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{1}}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{1}}\right)k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}+1},

for some
l_{k_{l}^{1}}
, where we order locations by their
k_{l}^{1}\Lambda_{l}
values (rather than just their
\Lambda_{l}
values). Let
\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}}
be the revenue-maximizing allocation given the fine vector
\textbf{k}^{1}
.

Now consider the setting corresponding to the fine vector
\textbf{k}^{2}=(k_{l}^{2})_{l\in L}
, which is such that for one location
l^{\prime}
,
k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}<k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}
, and fines for all other locations are the same as under the vector
\textbf{k}^{1}
. In this case, we construct an allocation
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
with at least the same objective as that under the fines
(k_{l}^{1})_{l\in L}
. In particular, there are three cases to consider: (i)
l^{\prime}>l_{k_{l}^{1}+1}
, (ii)
l^{\prime}<l_{k_{l}^{1}+1}
, and (iii)
l^{\prime}=l_{k_{l}^{1}+1}
.

Case (i):
Let
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}=\bm{\sigma}^{k^{1}}
. This implies that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{k}^{2})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})
, where (a) follows by the optimality of
\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}}
under the fine
\textbf{k}^{2}
and (b) follows as
l^{\prime}>l_{k_{l}^{1}+1}
.

Case (ii):
Let
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\sigma_{l}^{\textbf{k}^{1}}
for all
l\neq l^{\prime}
and let
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}^{\prime}=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}<\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}
. In this case, we have that:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{k}^{2}),

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k_{1}^{2}}k_{1}^{2}\Lambda_{1}+\ldots+\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}+\ldots+\frac{d_{l_{k_{l}^{2}}}}{d_{l_{k_{l}^{2}}}+k_{l_{k_{1}^{2}}}}k_{l_{k_{l}^{2}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{2}}}

\displaystyle+\left(R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{2}}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{2}}\right)k_{l_{k_{l}^{2}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{2}}+1},

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{1}}{d_{1}+k_{1}^{1}}k_{1}^{1}\Lambda_{1}+\ldots+\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}+\ldots+\frac{d_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}}{d_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}+k_{l_{k_{1}^{1}}}}k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}

\displaystyle+\left(R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{1}}]\backslash l^{\prime}}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{1}}-\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}\right)k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}},

\displaystyle=Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})+\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}-\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}-\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}+\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}k_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}^{1}\Lambda_{l_{k_{l}^{1}}}

Next, we compute the derivative of the latter function
f=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}
on the right-hand-side of the above term to get:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial f}{\partial k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}-\frac{k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}d_{l^{\prime}}}{(d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}}=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}}{(d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2})^{2}}\geq 0,

which implies that the additional term on the right hand side of the above inequality is non-negative, i.e.,
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})
.

Case (iii):
We consider two cases: (a)
R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{1}}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{1}}\leq\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}
and (b)
R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{1}}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{1}}>\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}
. In case (a), we let
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}=\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}}
, i.e., it clearly follows that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\prime},\textbf{k}^{2})\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})
, where the final inequality follows as
k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}>k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}
. Thus, consider case (b), where we let
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
be such that
\sigma_{l}^{\prime}=\sigma_{l}^{\textbf{k}^{1}}
for all
l\neq l^{\prime}
and let
\sigma_{l^{\prime}}=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}
. In this case, we get that:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})+\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}-\left(R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{1}}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{1}}\right)k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}

Without loss of generality, we consider the case when
R-\sum_{l\in[l_{k_{l}^{1}}]}\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k_{l}^{1}}=\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}
, as we are only interested in infinitesimal changes, as we would always be in case (a) otherwise for small enough change in the fine. Thus, the above term reduces to:

\displaystyle Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})

\displaystyle\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})+\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{2}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}-\frac{d_{l^{\prime}}}{d_{l^{\prime}}+k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}}k_{l^{\prime}}^{1}\Lambda_{l^{\prime}}.

However, from our earlier analysis, we know that the latter term in the right hand side is non-negative as the derivative of the corresponding function is non-negative in the fine. Thus, we have shown that
Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{2}},\textbf{k}^{2})\geq Q_{R}(\bm{\sigma}^{\textbf{k}^{1}},\textbf{k}^{1})
, which establishes our claim.

Note that in all the above cases
\bm{\sigma}^{\prime}
is feasible by construction as the fine is increased and thus the resource requirement for that location is lesser.

Thus, the above analysis implies that the revenue is monotonically non-decreasing in the fine for all locations. Consequently, the revenue optimal fines correspond to setting
k_{l}=\bar{k}
for all locations
l
.

Appendix E
Equilibrium Strategies of Administrator and Users in Contract Game

In this section, we study the strategies of the administrator and users in our contract game, given a contract parameter
\alpha\in[0,1]
. To simplify exposition and elucidate the main ideas of this contract game, we focus on the setting with homogeneous users at each location, and, following ideas developed in Section
5
, note that our proposed framework and results can be naturally generalized to the setting with heteregeneous users as well.

In studying the equilibrium strategies of the administrator and users, we first note that the problem of computing equilibria of this contract game is NP-hard, as solving the bi-level Program (
7a
)-(
7b
) is NP-hard, in general. The proof of this claim follows almost entirely analogously to the proof of Theorem
2
. In particular, as in the proof of Theorem
2
, we reduce from an instance of the partition problem and consider an instance of the contract game that is akin to the instance in the proof of Theorem
2
and where the number of fraudulent users
\Lambda_{l}=\delta
for all locations
l
is a small constant. In this setting, Objective (
7a
) is dominated by the welfare term for a sufficiently large
\alpha\in[0,1]
, e.g.,
\alpha=1
. Thus, on constructing an instance akin to that in the proof of Theorem
2
and where the number of fraudulent users
\Lambda
at each location is a small constant, the remainder of the proof follows from an almost entirely analogous line of reasoning to the proof of Theorem
2
; thus, we omit the remaining proof details for brevity.

Given the hardness of solving Problem (
7a
)-(
7b
), we now present a variant of a greedy algorithm to compute an administrator strategy with an approximation guarantee to the optimal solution of Problem (
7a
)-(
7b
) for any given contract parameter
\alpha\in[0,1]
. To this end, we first note that the best-response strategy
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)
of users at a given location
l
, corresponding to the solution of the lower-level Problem (
7b
), given a contract parameter
\alpha
and an administrator strategy
\bm{\sigma}
, is

\displaystyle y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)=\begin{cases}0,&\text{if }\sigma_{l}>\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\text{ or }\left(\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\text{ and }(k\Lambda_{l}+\alpha p_{l})\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}\leq\alpha p_{l}\right),\\
1,&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

(41)

Notice that as with our earlier studied best-response function of users in the revenue and payoff maximization settings, when
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
, any
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)\in[0,1]
is a best-response for users at location
l
. However, at the threshold
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
, we let
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)
take on the value zero or one depending on whether the administrator’s revenue corresponding to location
l
, given by
\alpha p_{l}
, when
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)=0
is smaller than the administrator’s revenue at location
l
, given by
(\Lambda_{l}+\alpha p_{l})\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
, when
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)=1
or not. Moreover, in the case when
\sigma_{l}=\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}
and the administrator is indifferent between the outcomes corresponding to
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)=1
or
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)=0
, i.e.,
(k\Lambda_{l}+\alpha v_{l})\frac{d_{l}}{d_{l}+k}=\alpha p_{l}
, we set
y_{l}(\bm{\sigma},\alpha)=0
, as this maximizes the payoff of the principal. Thus, the best-response function of users in Equation (
41
) is in alignment with the notion of strong Stackelberg equilibria
[
34
]
.

Having presented the best-response function of users in our studied contract game, we now present Algorithm
8
,
Contract-Greedy
, which extends our greedy-like algorithmic approach for the welfare maximization setting (see Algorithm
2
